# Chapter 1: The Alpha's Burden

The boundary markers had been moved again.

Victor knelt in the damp soil at the northern edge of Blackwood territory, running his fingers along the half-buried stone that marked the western perimeter. It sat three feet east of where it should have been. Three feet. To anyone else, nothing. To an Alpha, it was a declaration.

He stood and wiped his hands on his jeans, scanning the tree line. Douglas firs rose on all sides, ancient, dense, their needles sharp against the overcast sky. The forest here was thick enough to swallow sound. Wolves had grown up in this country. Shifted in it. Died in it. The land knew them the way a mother knows a child's footsteps, even in the dark.

Not this time.

Something was walking the boundary. Something that didn't belong.

"Alpha."

Victor didn't turn. He knew the voice, Samuel LaBlanc, the pack's best tracker. Quiet, methodical, and loyal to a fault. The kind of warrior who would take a blade for the pack without blinking.

"Report," Victor said.

Samuel approached through the brush, his dark hair damp with mist, eyes already scanning the tree line. "I picked up traces an hour ago. Wolf — but not ours. Not Blackwood. Someone's been marking our territory, but they're not doing it from the outside."

Victor's jaw tightened. "From the inside."

"Yeah. Whoever did this knew the patrol routes. They walked them." Samuel hesitated. "I also caught a scent that isn't wolf. It's old. Wrong. Like… standing next to a grave that's been dug up."

Victor didn't answer. He didn't need to. He could feel it, a pressure at the edge of perception, like a room slowly filling with water and no one willing to name it.

"What about the missing hunter?" Victor asked.

Samuel's face darkened. "No sign of Marcus. No tracks leading out. Nothing. He was on the northern boundary at dawn. Radio went silent at twelve-thirteen. No body, no blood, no—"

Victor held up a hand. He didn't need the rest. Marcus was one of his lieutenants. A good warrior. Loyal. The kind of man who laughed too loud and fed strays from his pocket. If he was gone, it meant one of two things: something ancient and hungry had taken him, or something else entirely had.

Neither option was acceptable.

"Mark the boundary with fresh cairns," Victor said. "Double the patrol schedule. I want eyes on the northern sector around the clock. No one goes out past the markers alone."

"Victor—" Samuel started, then caught himself. He'd only called Victor by his first name a handful of times in the five years he'd served the pack. It was a habit born of urgency, not insolence. "David's already pushing for a counter-patrol. He wants to go south and see if anyone's been crossing from the Hanley side."

Victor exhaled slowly. The Hanley pack. Another wolf faction, three hundred miles south in Louisiana swampland, but wolves were nomads by instinct and the boundary between their territory and Blackwood's was a negotiation that never ended. If the Hanleys were testing the northern perimeter, they were looking for weakness. And Victor had to decide whether to show strength or hide it.

"No," he said. "Not yet. We don't know who's moving or where. If we send a counter-patrol south, we leave the north exposed. I'd rather look stupid than vulnerable."

Samuel nodded. "Understood."

He hesitated, then added, "The pack is asking questions. Josiah says you haven't slept in three nights. Emila says you haven't eaten properly in four. They're worried."

Victor almost smiled. Almost. "Tell Emila I'm fine. Tell Josiah I'm not dying. And tell the pack that Alpha doesn't answer to their concern — he answers to their safety."

Samuel's mouth twitched. "That's not what you told me to say."

"Do it anyway."

When Samuel had disappeared into the trees, Victor stood alone by the moved boundary stone and stared north. The forest stretched for miles, dark, quiet, wrong. He could feel it pressing against the edge of his awareness like a hand on glass. If he squinted, he could almost see it: a faint shimmer, like heat rising from asphalt, except this wasn't heat. It was absence. A place where the world thinned to nothing.

He didn't tell Samuel about that part. The pack didn't need to know their Alpha could *feel* something wrong pressing against the edge of the world, not yet. They'd panic. They'd look for someone to blame. They'd demand action they weren't ready for.

Victor turned his back on the boundary and started the walk home.

---

The compound rose from the valley floor like it had grown there, stone and timber, wide windows catching the weak morning light. It sat in a secluded hollow surrounded by mountains on three sides, the valley opening only to the north where the boundary markers stood guard. The Blackwood pack had built it fifty years ago, when Cyrus, Victor's father, the Alpha before him, the man who had earned the position through blood and bond, decided the pack needed a permanent home.

Victor walked through the main gate without slowing. Two warriors at attention, Raine Blackwell and Marco Hayes, both straightened as he passed. Raine's hand went to the knife at her belt out of habit. Marco's mouth opened to make a joke, then closed when he saw Victor's face.

Evening was approaching. The sky had shifted from gray to a muted gold, and the air smelled of pine and damp earth and the woodsmoke from the central hearth. The compound's heart was the great hall, a long room with a vaulted ceiling, stone floor, and a fireplace large enough to stand in. That's where the pack gathered. That's where the food was. That's where the questions would start.

He had time.

Victor went to his quarters, a small room off the main hall, spartan but functional. Bed, desk, a shelf of dog-eared field manuals and pack histories. No personal items. No photos. No sign that a man lived there beyond the worn edges of the mattress and the knife he kept locked in the bottom drawer.

The room was on the ground floor. His father's quarters were on the second floor, larger, with a proper study and a window overlooking the valley. Victor had refused to move in the day Cyrus died. "Not yet," he'd told Josiah. "I haven't earned it." He was still telling himself that three years later. Every time he walked past the second-floor window, he felt the weight of the space beside him, not literal, but emotional. The empty bed. The empty desk. The empty Alpha. He wasn't Cyrus. He wasn't ready to take the room.

He stripped off his jacket, rolled his shoulders, and let out a breath he'd been holding since dawn. His left shoulder ached, an old scar from a pack dispute three years ago, when a rogue wolf had tried to challenge him. The rogue had lost. Victor had won. The scar didn't.

He washed his face, splashed cold water over his hands, and stared at his reflection in the small mirror above the sink. Dark hair. Gray eyes that shifted toward amber when his emotions ran high. Lines around his mouth from frowning more than smiling. He looked twenty-eight, but he felt forty.

The first shift had come on his sixteenth birthday, the way it always did. He remembered the pain, bones cracking and reforming, muscles tearing and knitting, the world shifting scale and color and scent until the human half of his mind was swallowed by something wild and ancient and *hungry*. He'd collapsed in the clearing behind the compound, shaking, terrified, and his father, Cyrus, the old Alpha, had knelt beside him and said:

*"You are a wolf now. That means you are part of something bigger than yourself. You will never be alone again."*

He hadn't been alone then. He wasn't alone now. But being part of something bigger didn't mean the weight was lighter. It meant someone else could share it. And his father was gone, killed by the dark three years ago, defending the northern boundary, and Victor had been too far away to help.

The Alpha position hadn't been handed to him, he'd earned it, bloodied knuckles and broken bones and sleepless nights included. But earning it and believing he deserved it were two different things. His father had built this pack from the ground up, held it together through war, through drought, through three rival packs trying to take their land. He had died a hero. Victor had survived his father. That wasn't the same thing. And every night, when the compound went quiet and the fire burned low, Victor lay awake and wondered if he'd made the right call that night. If he'd been faster, stronger, smarter, if he'd just been enough.

Victor opened the drawer, checked his knife, and closed it again.

He stood, straightened his jacket, and walked out of the quarters toward the great hall. He had time before the council. Time to think. Time to prepare.

But even as he walked, he felt it, the weight of the boundary on his shoulders, the missing hunter weighing in his gut, the pack's quiet worry humming beneath every conversation in the compound. He was the Alpha. He carried all of it.

And for now, that was enough.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 2: The Council

The great hall was already half-full when Victor arrived.

He entered through the side door and paused at the threshold, letting the room settle around him. It always took a moment, thirty seconds, maybe a minute, for the murmuring conversations to die down, for heads to turn, for the pack's energy to shift from scattered individuals to something organized and expectant. He was used to it. It didn't make it any easier.

He didn't sit. He stood at the head of the long stone table and let the silence do the work.

David was the first to break it.

"The northern boundary," he said, leaning forward with his elbows on the table. "We need to push forward. If someone's marking our land, we show them we don't take kindly to trespassers."

Perseus shook his head. "That's exactly what they want. They want us to react. If we send a patrol south, they know we're spooked. If we send too many, they know we're scared."

"We're not scared," David snapped.

"No," Victor said quietly. "But we don't need to prove it by walking into a trap."

David's mouth pressed into a thin line. He didn't like being told no, nobody liked it, but he knew better than to push Victor in front of the whole pack. He sat back.

Victor let the quiet settle, then turned to face the room properly. He stood straight, hands resting on the back of the chair at the head of the table, and spoke.

"I'm going to be direct. The boundary markers on the north have been moved again — this time by three feet. Samuel found traces that don't belong to our pack. The scent is old and wrong. And we're missing a hunter."

A murmur rippled through the room. Marcus LaBlanc's name hung in the air like a weight.

"I don't know what happened to him," Victor continued. "I don't know who or what took him. But something is out there. And it's not just testing the boundary — it's testing us."

Josiah Amos sat at Victor's right hand, his weathered face calm, his eyes sharp. He had been Cyrus's advisor. Now he was Victor's. He hadn't said much yet, but his silence carried weight. When he spoke, the room leaned in.

"These things have happened before," he said, his voice low and deliberate. "In my memory, there were signs — animal behavior shifting, the Veil feeling thin, the cold coming at the wrong time of year. Always, it preceded something bigger. Something we didn't see until it was too late."

Emila Sterling, the pack's Omega, sat at the far end of the table. Her warm brown eyes tracked Victor's face as she listened. She was small but unshakeable, with the kind of presence that made people bring her their problems and stay for the answers. The pack's healer. Its emotional center. The most politically astute person in the room, though she'd never claim it.

Josiah caught her eye. She gave a small nod, the same nod Cyrus had given him, a thousand times, in a thousand meetings. The nod that meant *I hear you, and I don't want to hear it.*

"The Veil." Perseus's expression hardened. He shook his head slowly. "The Veil doesn't just *thin*. It's been holding for centuries. What exactly are you saying happened?"

"I'm saying the pack has felt the thinning for weeks," Victor said. "I can feel it. It's like standing in a room where the air pressure drops right before a storm. You know something's coming. You just don't know what."

David frowned. "The Veil is the Moon's doing. She maintains it. If it's thinning, it's because she's allowing it."

"Maybe," Josiah said. "Or maybe it's thinning because something is pulling at it from the other side."

The room went quiet. That was the wrong thing to say. Everyone in the room knew what was on the other side of the Veil. That was why the Veil existed.

Emila cleared her throat. "Before we spiral into fear," she said, her voice even and measured, "let's focus on what we can control. We have a missing hunter. We have boundary disturbances. We have a pack that's nervous. We need to address those things, not the ghosts we think might be coming."

Victor nodded at her. "Agreed. Here's what we're doing. Patrols on the northern boundary are expanding immediately — double the schedule, triple the watchers. No one goes out past the markers alone. Samuel, you're leading the north sector. I want boots on the ground around the clock."

Samuel LaBlanc, Victor's best tracker, nodded. His jaw was tight, his hands clenched in fists at his sides. His brother was out there somewhere. He wasn't going to rest until he was found. "Understood."

"Perseus, reinforce the boundary stones with fresh cairns. Mark them with pack sigils. If someone's walking our territory, I want them to know we're watching."

Perseus inclined his head. "Consider it done."

"And David—" Victor turned to him. David's hands were still clenched on his knees, his eyes bright with the urge to act. "You're on the south side. Monitor the Hanley border. I don't want conflict, but I also don't want us blindsided. Keep your men sharp. Keep your head cool."

David's jaw worked. "Cool isn't going to be easy with Hanley pressing on our southern flank."

"I know."

David exhaled, frustrated. He wanted to fight. They all did, deep down. Shifters were predators by nature, and predators didn't like not knowing where the next meal was coming from. But Victor had been Alpha long enough to know that not every problem could be solved with teeth.

Josiah stood. His chair scraped against the stone floor, and the sound cut through the room like a bell. He was older than anyone else at the table, older than Cyrus, probably older than any of them would be, and when he stood, people moved.

"The old ones said the dark would return," he said, looking at the entire room, not just Victor. "I thought they were just stories. Stories meant to frighten pups. But stories that survive this long aren't just stories. They're warnings. And warnings don't come without reason."

He looked at Victor. "We need to prepare for something we can't fight with swords and teeth."

Victor met his gaze. "Then tell us what to prepare for."

Josiah's expression didn't change. But something in his eyes softened, the way it always did when he was about to say something that didn't fit in a pack meeting.

"Not today," he said. "Today, we hold the line. Tomorrow, we think."

Victor didn't press. Josiah had never been wrong about timing. Not once.

He looked back at the room. "Any other concerns?"

Silence.

"Good. Dismissed."

The pack members filed out, talking quietly, faces tight with concern. Marco clapped David on the shoulder as he passed, and David shrugged him off. Becca lingered by the door, looking at her brother, looking at Victor, not sure whether to stay or go.

Emila found Victor at the head of the table after everyone else had left.

"You haven't eaten," she said.

"I'm fine."

"Victor." She used his name — not Alpha, not sir — and he looked up to find her standing in front of him with a plate of food. Roast beef, bread, vegetables. Homemade. Her way of caring.

"I'm not—"

"Sit down." She didn't raise her voice. She didn't need to. Emila was Omega. When she said *sit down*, the pack sat down.

He sat.

She set the plate in front of him and leaned against the table, watching him eat. "You're carrying it alone again."

"I'm fine."

"You're not." Her voice was soft but firm. "You're the Alpha. You think that means you have to be strong all the time. You think that means you can't show fear. But you're not a damn wall, Victor. You're a person. And people aren't built to carry everything by themselves."

He looked up. Her eyes were kind, but there was steel underneath. Emila knew him better than anyone in the pack. She had been there since he was a pup, since before Cyrus died, before the Alpha bond shifted to him. She had seen him fall. She had seen him get up. She had also seen him bleed.

"I'm not—"

"Save it." She pushed the plate closer. "Eat. Then we'll talk about what you're not telling me."

He ate. He didn't finish it. But he ate enough.

When she left, he sat alone in the great hall as the sun went down and the fire crackled low. He could hear the pack moving through the compound, boots on stone, voices in the distance, the low murmur of lives lived together for centuries. He was surrounded by family.

And he had never felt more alone.

---

The balcony was small, one of the few things in the compound that wasn't designed to impress. It jutted from the second-floor wall, barely wide enough for two people to stand side by side, overlooking the valley and the darkening tree line beyond. Victor had found it by accident three years ago, the night after Cyrus's funeral, when he'd climbed out the window and stood there in the snow until his feet went numb.

He came up here now to think. To breathe. To pretend, for a few minutes, that he wasn't the Alpha.

The sky was dark. The first stars were visible, faint pinpricks against the overcast blanket. The moon wasn't out yet, not for a few more hours. But even without it, he could feel it. The pull. Like a thread running from his chest up into the sky, tugging at something he couldn't name.

He stared north.

The forest stretched for miles, dark, quiet, wrong. He could feel the pressure pressing against the edge of his awareness, like a hand on glass. If he squinted, he could almost see it: a faint shimmer, like heat rising from asphalt, except this wasn't heat. It was absence. A place where the world thinned to nothing.

And beneath it all, beneath the pressure and the wrongness, beneath the fear and the weight of a pack's survival on his shoulders, there was something else. A sound. Not a real sound, there was nothing in the air to create it. But it was there, deep in his bones, in the part of him that had been wolf before it had been human, older than language and older than memory.

The moon was calling.

He didn't know why. He didn't know what it wanted. But for the first time in three years, he felt something that wasn't the weight. Something that wasn't the fear.

Something that felt, for just a moment, like purpose.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 3: Amelia's Day

The morning started the way mornings always did — with silence. Not the deep, empty silence of a house where nobody lived, but the quiet hum of a place that was entirely hers. Sunlight came through the kitchen window in a slanted rectangle, catching dust motes that drifted like slow snow. The kettle sat on the stove, the stove sat on the counter, the counter sat next to a window that looked out at the tree line and beyond that, the edge of a forest nobody in town talked about unless they were drunk.

Amelia Wren stood at that counter with a chipped blue mug in her hand, still as someone who'd lived alone too long.

Her cat, a scarred slate-black cat named Luna with orange eyes who looked permanently unimpressed by everything, jumped onto the counter and stared at her. She set the mug down and opened a tin of fish. Luna ate like she was doing her a favor.

She poured tea, carried it to the table, and sat. On her left wrist, half-hidden under the cuff of her sweater sleeve, a crescent moon tattoo pressed against her skin. Inked by her grandmother when she was twelve, *"so you remember that you come from someone who knows how to read the sky."* Amelia had been fifteen when her grandmother died. She was twenty-six now. The tattoo was the only thing she had left of her that felt sacred.

She drank her tea. She ate toast. She looked at the window. The trees were still. Too still for this time of morning, the birds should have been singing by now, but the forest had been quiet for weeks. Not peaceful quiet. Holding-its-breath quiet.

She told herself it was nothing.

---

The clinic wasn't much, a small stone building on the edge of town that looked as if it had been there forever. The sign out front said *Amelia Wren, Herbalist & Healer* in letters her grandmother had painted decades ago, and below that, in smaller script, *By Appointment, or If You're Bleeding, Come Anyway.*

She didn't need to tell anyone she was open. People knew. Word traveled through this part of the county faster than cell service ever could, and word was that Amelia Wren could fix what the doctor couldn't, or wouldn't.

The first patient of the day was Mrs. Gable, a sixty-year-old with arthritis in her hands and stubbornness in her spine. She came in with a tin of homemade cookies and a complaint that the weather was making her knuckles ache. Amelia made her a salve from comfrey and wintergreen, rubbed it into her hands with firm, practiced fingers, and sent her off with two tablets of willow bark for the pain. Mrs. Gable hugged her on the way out.

"Your grandmother would be proud," she said.

Amelia smiled. "She'd tell you to stop leaning on your right side when you walk."

Mrs. Gable laughed. "She always did see too much."

After Mrs. Gable left, the second patient arrived. He was a young hunter, maybe twenty-two, lean and windburned, with a gash on his forearm that was definitely not from a tree branch. The edges were too smooth. The shape was wrong. He said he'd fallen on a rock. She'd heard that story three times this week.

She cleaned the wound, packed it with honey and yarrow, wrapped it tightly. He winced but didn't pull away. When she was done, he pressed a five-dollar bill into her hand and muttered a thank-you that sounded more like a prayer than a word.

"What happened out there?" she asked quietly.

He looked up. His eyes were wide, dark, and young. "Nothing. Just... lost my footing. It's nothing."

She didn't push. She'd learned that lesson early, some people didn't want answers. They just wanted the bleeding to stop so they could go back to pretending everything was normal.

She watched him leave through the window. He walked fast, eyes on the ground, shoulders hunched like he was carrying something heavier than his pack. He looked back once at the tree line behind him. He was probably right to.

Amelia washed her hands, locked the front door, and sat down at her desk. She opened a notebook, blank pages waiting for her own notes, her own remedies, her own thoughts. She didn't write in it much. Most of what she knew lived in her hands, in the feel of a pulse under her fingers, in the way a wound looked and smelled and bled. But sometimes, usually at night, when the house was quiet and Luna was curled on her feet and the silence felt less like peace and more like absence, she wrote things down.

Today she wrote: *Three hunters with strange wounds in one week. All from the north side of the boundary. All saying it was an accident. None of them talking about what they saw.*

She closed the notebook. She didn't read it again. She didn't need to. She remembered everything.

---

The afternoon stretched. Two more patients, a broken finger, a sunburn. She tended to both. She ate lunch standing over the sink, a bowl of soup that her neighbor had dropped off. She drank water. She looked out the window every ten minutes, even though there was nothing to see. The sun was low, the light was turning gold, and the forest was still holding its breath.

She was wiping down the counter when the bell above the door chimed.

She turned.

A woman in her late sixties, gray hair pinned up, carrying a canvas bag. She didn't look like a patient. She looked like a person who brought things.

"Evening, Mrs. Calloway," Amelia said.

Mrs. Calloway set the bag on the counter. "Thought you might be hungry. You always look peaky by afternoon."

Amelia smiled despite herself. "You always bring me soup."

"You always need soup." Mrs. Calloway's eyes twinkled. She reached into the bag and pulled out a small packet wrapped in paper. "And I brought you something else. Your grandmother gave this to me before she passed. Said if I ever saw someone who needed it, I should give it to them."

Amelia took the packet. It was heavy, warm, wrapped in twine. She didn't open it. She didn't need to, the paper smelled like fresh pancakes, bacon, and fried eggs, and something she couldn't name but recognized in her bones.

"Tell me you didn't give this to anyone else," she said.

Mrs. Calloway shook her head. "Just you. And only because the time was right."

Amelia's throat felt tight. "I don't— I don't know what to say."

"Say you'll take care of yourself," Mrs. Calloway said. She reached across the counter and squeezed Amelia's wrist, right over the moon tattoo. "You're not as invisible as you think you are, child."

After Mrs. Calloway left, Amelia sat in the empty clinic and held the packet without opening it. She stared at her hands. They were steady. They had always been steady. Her grandmother taught her to hold a scalpel with her pinky finger raised and her thumb anchored. She taught her to read a pulse like it was a language. She taught her that healing wasn't just about fixing what was broken, it was about understanding what had caused the break in the first place.

She didn't understand what was causing the breaks now.

She put the packet in her pocket. She stood up. She locked the door behind her and walked home.

---

Her cottage was small, one story, stone walls, a thatched roof, a garden out back that was more weeds than herbs because she'd been meaning to get to it. It sat on the edge of the woods, just far enough away that people didn't think she was strange for living there. Close enough that she could hear the forest at night.

She let Luna in first, locked the door, and set the kettle on. She changed out of her work clothes into a soft cotton shirt and loose pants, made tea, and ate a bowl of broth. She sat at her small kitchen table and let her hands rest on the wood grain, feeling the warmth of the cup through her palms.

The house was quiet. It was always quiet. Not peaceful. Empty. A space where somebody else's footsteps should echo. A voice that should answer back. A hand that should reach across the table and touch hers.

She didn't think about it much. She'd learned, over the years, to just sit with the silence and let it be what it was. It was calmer that way. Less painful.

Her grandmother had tried to tell her she wasn't built for it. *"You feel too deeply, Amelia. That's not a flaw. That's a gift. But if you keep bottling it up, it'll eat you alive."*

She'd spent her whole life being told she was too much. Too intense. Too emotional. Too sensitive. Too intense about her work, too sensitive about her feelings, too much of everything for people who wanted less. Sometimes, late at night, when she was walking home through the dark, she'd catch figures at the edge of the woods. Just shapes, barely visible. They never spoke to her. They just watched. Like she was something they were trying to figure out.

So she'd learned to live apart. She'd learned to make herself small. She'd learned to be competent and self-reliant and quiet, and to let people decide for themselves whether she was interesting enough to talk to. Most of them decided she wasn't.

It is fine.

You are fine.

Everything is fine.

She said it often enough to almost believe it. Sometimes she wondered if it was enough.

---

The kettle whistled. She poured another cup. She carried it to the window and stood there, looking out at the trees.

The moon was out. A sliver, pale and thin, just beginning to rise behind the ridge. She watched it for a long moment, letting the quiet settle around her. The forest was still quiet. Luna had jumped onto the table and was watching her with the same orange eyes she'd worn since she was old enough to feed her.

She raised her mug in a silent toast. "To another day," she said.

Luna blinked slowly. It was the closest thing to agreement she'd get.

She set her mug down and turned away from the window. She was about to head to the bedroom when it hit her.

A sharp, sudden pull in her chest. A hook, yanking.

She froze. Her breath caught. Her hands, her hands started tingling, a strange, electric sensation that crawled up from her fingertips and spread through her arms like ice water.

She gripped the edge of the table. Her eyes went wide.

"What—"

It wasn't pain. Gravity had shifted. Everything in her body was tilting toward something she couldn't see. She looked around the room. The same room she'd been standing in three seconds ago. The same table. The same window. The same trees outside.

But the pull didn't stop. It didn't even ease.

It got stronger.

Her chest ached. Her hands vibrated. Her heart hammered against her ribs like it was trying to get out, or get somewhere, or to someone.

She took a step forward. Then another. She didn't know where she was going. She just knew that standing still was worse than whatever this was.

She walked to the door. She opened it. The night air hit her face, cool and clean and smelling of pine and something else, something she couldn't name but recognized—a dream she'd had, long ago, that she couldn't place.

She stepped onto the porch.

The pull didn't change. It didn't point her toward the cottage, toward the garden, toward town. It pointed her toward the forest. Toward the boundary. Toward the dark trees that held the edge of the world.

And for the first time in her life, Amelia Wren felt something that wasn't loneliness.

It was a hook. It was a thread. It was a pull in her chest that was stronger than any fear, any instinct, any rational thought.

She told herself it was nothing.

She told herself it was nerves; it was exhaustion; it was the weather.

She stood on her porch, her hands shaking, her heart racing, and watched the trees in the dark, wondering why the unnamed feeling felt more real than anything she'd ever known.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 4: The Edge of the World

The pull didn't let her sleep.

It wasn't a pain — not exactly. But it was there, persistent and low, like a hum that lived behind her ribs and wouldn't quiet. She lay on her back, staring at the ceiling, trying to figure out what it was that she was feeling. Something. Someone. The word didn't fit, but neither did the ache in her chest.

She was tired. The kind of tired that comes from lying still too long, from letting your body do nothing while your mind works itself into a knot. She got up, made tea, and fed Luna. She sat at the kitchen table and let the quiet settle around her like a blanket she couldn't shake.

The pull didn't go away.

---

The morning was cool and damp with fog, thick and low over the ground. Amelia walked through the tall grass toward the edge of the woods, a canvas satchel slung over her shoulder, her boots sinking slightly into the damp earth. The boundary was a line she'd crossed a hundred times without thinking about it. It was just a fence post and a rusted wire marking the edge of her garden and the beginning of someone else's land. But sometimes, usually at dusk, when the light was low and the shadows were long, she could feel the difference. The forest didn't just start at the boundary. Something changed there, something subtle, like the air itself getting heavier.

Today was no different.

She knelt by a patch of yarrow growing along the fence line, careful not to damage the roots as she harvested. The plant was sturdy, easy to find, and useful for everything from wound packing to tea. She liked yarrow. It was unpretentious. It grew wherever you put it, and it always came back.

Her hands moved automatically. Gather, snip, tuck into the satchel. Gather, snip, tuck.

Then the pull hit her.

It was sharp, sudden, and stronger than before — a hook sinking into her chest and yanking. She stopped. Her hands froze mid-air. Her breath caught.

"Damn it," she muttered.

It wasn't the same as last night. Last night had been a whisper. This was a shout. Her hands, her hands started tingling, the same electric sensation crawling up from her fingertips and spreading through her arms like ice water. She stared at them. They were trembling. Not from cold. From something else.

She looked around. The fog was rolling in from the north. The forest was gray and shapeless beyond the fence line. She couldn't see anything through the mist. But she could feel it, the pull, the tension, the invisible thread stretching from her chest toward the trees.

She told herself it was nothing.

She told herself it was nerves. It was the damp. It was her imagination.

The tingling didn't stop. It got stronger.

She straightened up. Her heart was beating fast. Her palms were damp. She pressed one hand to her chest, like she could press the feeling back down. It didn't work.

She stood there for a long moment, looking at the fog, listening to the silence, and wondering what the hell was happening to her.

---

Miles away, on the northern edge of Blackwood territory, Victor reined his horse to a stop.

He'd been riding the patrol route for an hour, a slow, methodical sweep along the boundary markers, checking for disturbance, for scent marks, for anything out of place. The morning was clear, the sky pale and bright, the air crisp with the first hint of autumn. It should have been a normal patrol. It should have been nothing.

Then it hit him.

A sharp, sudden sensation, not pain, not fear, but something between the two. His chest tightened. His hands gripped the reins so hard his knuckles went white. His heart hammered against his ribs like something was trying to get out.

He looked around. Tree line to tree line, nothing but the road, the forest, and the pale morning sky. No movement. No scent. No threat.

But the feeling didn't fade. It didn't weaken. It stayed, steady and unwavering, like a compass needle locked on true north.

He'd felt things like this before. The pull of the pack, the weight of the Alpha bond, the call of the full moon. But this was different. This was personal. His body was reacting to something his mind couldn't explain.

He exhaled slowly. He told himself it was the same feeling from last night, the moon pull, the pressure. He told himself a lot of things. Most of them were lies.

His hands were tingling.

He looked at them. His fingers were spread across the reins, his palms flat, his knuckles pressed tight against the leather. And they were trembling. Not much. Just enough to notice. Just enough to be strange.

He'd never felt anything like this.

He didn't know that Amelia was three miles away, on the other end of whatever this was. He didn't know that she was standing by the boundary line, her hand pressed against her chest, her heart racing, wondering what was happening to her. He didn't know any of that.

All he knew was that something had shifted in the world, and he couldn't explain it.

---

Amelia packed up her herbs and started walking back. She didn't know why. She just knew she couldn't stand still anymore. Every time she stopped, the pull got stronger. Every time she moved, it stayed the same, present, insistent, unrelenting. She told herself she was going home. She told herself she was going to make lunch. In her mind, she knew both of those things were not true.

The tingling in her hands didn't stop. It followed her back through the tall grass, through the fog, past the rusted fence post and into the yard of her cottage. She sat on the porch steps, her satchel on the ground beside her, and stared at her palms. They were still shaking. Still tingling. Still alive with something she couldn't name.

She looked up at the tree line. The fog had thinned. She could see the edge of the woods now, the dark trunks, the branches reaching up, the way the light hit the leaves and made them glow green-gold against the blue sky. The forest looked normal. It didn't look like anything that could make her heart beat like this.

But it did. Whatever it was, it was coming from there. From the boundary. From the edge of the world.

She closed her eyes, she pressed her hand to her chest and for the first time in her life, she felt something that wasn't loneliness, or fear, or the quiet ache of a life lived alone.

It was a hook. It was a thread. It was a pull toward something she couldn't see, couldn't name, or reach, but that she felt, deep in her bones, like it was the most important thing in the world.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 5: The Patrol

The morning started like any other — with the smell of coffee and the sound of boots on the porch, followed by a cough in the doorway.

Amelia's clinic smelled like the same things it always did — antiseptic, dried herbs, the faint scent of liniment from the jars lined along the shelves. Normal smells. Predictable smells. Safe.

She made herself a cup of coffee, opened the front door, and went to work.

But her hands were tingling.

She told herself it was nothing. It was the coffee. It was the damp. It was nerves from yesterday.

The tingling didn't stop.

---

The first patient was Mrs. Calloway, who'd dropped off soup last time and needed a new prescription for her blood pressure. Amelia wrote it out, handed it to her with a smile, and watched her leave through the window. The second was a young woman with a sprained wrist from slipping on the ice. The third was a farmer with a cut on his hand from a mower blade.

She treated all of them. She did everything right. She knew what she was doing.

But her hands were tingling. She told herself it would fade. It always faded. It had faded the night before, hadn't it?

It faded again by afternoon. But it didn't go away completely. It lingered, a low hum in the back of her palms. Like electricity after a storm — present but faint.

She told herself it was fine.

It wasn't fine.

---

Victor

Victor rode back to the compound after dark.

His horse was tired. So was he. He'd spent the last four hours riding the northern boundary, checking the markers, looking for signs, trying to find whatever it was that had moved his heart and his hands and his mind in a direction he couldn't control. He hadn't found anything. No tracks. No scent. No disturbance. Just the feeling, steady and persistent, like a compass needle that wouldn't settle.

He was exhausted. He was shaken. He'd been raised to believe that he understood the world, that he knew how things worked, that he could predict everything. None of that mattered anymore. Something had shifted in him and he didn't know how to fix it.

He reined his horse at the compound gate. The stone walls rose up around him, dark and familiar. The smell of woodsmoke and cooking food drifted from the great hall. He could hear voices, pack members, probably, sitting around tables, talking, laughing, living their lives like everything was normal.

It wasn't normal. Nothing was normal. But he was the Alpha, and Alphas didn't let the pack see them shaken.

He dismounted, tied his horse, and walked inside.

The great hall was warm and full of light. Fires crackled in the stone hearths. Pack members sat at long wooden tables, eating, talking, laughing. David was at the far end of the room, arm draped over the back of his chair, talking loud enough for half the room to hear. Perseus sat near the door, his expression unreadable, his eyes tracking every person who entered and left. He looked up the moment Victor walked in.

And he noticed.

Perseus had been trained to notice things. It was his job, to watch the pack, to read the room, to understand what people were saying and what they weren't. He noticed that Victor's hands were clenched at his sides. He noticed that his knuckles were white. He noticed that his jaw was tight, his shoulders tense, his eyes distant and unfocused.

Victor caught him looking. Perseus looked away.

"Alpha," Perseus said, his voice quiet. "You look like hell."

Victor didn't smile. He didn't frown. He just kept walking, past the tables, past the fires, past the pack, toward the head of the table where he was supposed to sit and eat and be normal and pretend that nothing was wrong.

"I'm fine," he said.

Perseus watched him go. He didn't believe him. But he didn't press. Not yet.

David noticed.

"Did the Alpha get spooked by a squirrel?" he said, loud enough for the whole room to hear. He grinned. "Because I can help you with that. I've got a trap set up. We can go out tomorrow. I'll catch it. You can put it down."

Laughter rippled through the room. David was joking, mostly.

Victor didn't laugh. He sat at the head of the table. He stared at the fire. He thought about the pull, the tingling, the feeling that something had shifted in the world and he couldn't explain it to anyone. He thought about the way his hands had been shaking on his horse, the way his heart had hammered against his ribs, the way the feeling had stayed with him all day, like a shadow he couldn't outrun.

He didn't tell anyone. He never told anyone.

David's smile faded. He looked at Perseus. Perseus looked back. They both knew something was wrong. They just didn't know what.

---

Amelia

That afternoon, Amelia sat at her kitchen table and touched the moon tattoo on her wrist.

It was something she'd never done before. She'd looked at it, of course she had, she'd looked at it a thousand times, admired the ink, remembered the night her grandmother had held her hand steady while the needle moved across her skin, but she'd never touched it. Not like this. Not with the deliberate intention of feeling it.

But her hand had moved without asking her. Her fingers had found the edge of her sleeve. Her thumb had traced the curve of the crescent moon, the little stars scattered around it, the faint line of her grandmother's handwriting that had been pressed into the design, *"Read the sky, and the sky will read you back."*

She'd never touched it before.

Today, her fingers burned.

It wasn't a lot, not a real burn, not a pain. But it was there, a sharp, sudden heat that crawled across her skin like the mark was alive, like it was responding to something she couldn't feel but that was definitely there.

She pulled her hand back. She stared at the tattoo. She touched it again.

Nothing.

She touched it a third time. Nothing.

She told herself it was nothing. It was her imagination. It was the coffee. It was nerves.

It didn't stop feeling warm for hours.

---

That night, Amelia dreamed of a wolf.

Not a real wolf, not small and furry and cute. This wolf was the size of a horse. Its fur was dark, almost black, but not quite, like storm clouds, like deep water, like the space between stars. Its eyes were amber, bright, sharp, intelligent, and they were looking right at her.

She didn't know where she was. She didn't know what she was doing in the dream. But she knew, with a certainty that was deeper than reason, that the wolf was not a threat. It was watching her. Not with hunger, not with fear, but with recognition.

The wolf took a step toward her.

She woke.

Her hands were tingling. Her chest was aching. Her heart was racing. And the dream was still there, the wolf, the amber eyes, the feeling of recognition, burning behind her eyelids like a photograph left too long in the sun.

She lay on her back. She stared at the ceiling. And she wondered why, for the first time in her life, she didn't want to be alone.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 6: The Missing Hunter

Marcus LaBlanc was gone.

Not injured. Not captured. Not anywhere anyone could find him.

Marcus LaBlanc was twenty-two, a young hunter who'd been assigned to patrol the northern boundary at dawn. His patrol report was the last thing he left behind, three lines of neat handwriting on the supply room clipboard, noting nothing out of the ordinary. The markers were standing. The trail was clear. The scent was fresh, leading north, and then it just ended.

No tracks after that. No blood. No struggle.

Just silence.

---

The emergency council was called before noon.

It was the third one in as many days. Victor stood at the head of the long wooden table, his hands flat against the grain, his jaw set, his eyes fixed on the map laid out before him. The northern boundary stretched along the edge of the map like a thin red line, and on that line, at a point marked by a small wooden post, someone had drawn a circle.

Marcus's patrol report had been the last thing recorded before his scent trail ended. No one knew what had happened after that. No one knew where he was.

David was already pacing.

"We need to send a team out," he said. "Right now. He's probably hurt — maybe he wandered off the trail, maybe he's unconscious. We need to find him before nightfall."

Perseus sat at the far end of the table, his expression unreadable. He'd been quiet this whole time. But he wasn't ignoring it. He was watching, watching Victor, watching David, watching the way the pack shifted around the table, waiting for him to make a decision.

Perseus knew what was at stake. He'd learned the hard way in his first year as a hunter, when he'd made a mistake that cost a friend his life, that rushing into the unknown was a fast way to die. And he wasn't going to make the same mistake twice.

"We don't know what we're dealing with," he said. His voice was calm. Quiet. But the room went still when he spoke. "No tracks. No blood. No sign of a struggle. This isn't an animal attack. This isn't an accident. Something took him. And we don't know what it is."

David stopped pacing. He looked at Perseus. "Then we send a team to find out."

"We send a team," Perseus said, "and we all end up missing. Or worse."

The room went quiet.

Perseus looked at Victor. He thought of Josiah, sitting in the corner of the great hall, listening to the stories the young ones told and pretending not to care. The old hunter had said it once, years ago, when no one was paying attention: *"The old ones said the dark would return. Stories meant to frighten pups. But stories that survive this long aren't just stories. They're warnings."*

Perseus had dismissed it then. He wasn't sure he could dismiss it now.

"I'm ordering a full search," Victor said. His voice was steady. Firm. Commanding. "But David — no one goes alone. Two hunters minimum. Three if we can spare them. And they don't go past the boundary markers. They follow Marcus's scent to the last point and they come back."

David opened his mouth to argue. He closed it and nodded. He was disappointed, but he understood.

Perseus watched him go. He looked at Victor. He didn't say anything. But his look said everything.

*You're going to need more than a search party.*

---

Amelia

Amelia heard about the missing hunter the way you heard about things in a small town, not through official announcements, but through whispers in the market, through patients who came in with rumors, the way people looked at her when they thought she wasn't paying attention.

Mrs. Calloway mentioned it first. She came in for her blood pressure refill, stood at the counter, and said it casually, like she was talking about the weather.

"Pack's nervous," she said. "One of their hunters gone missing. Out past the northern boundary."

Amelia nodded. She didn't say anything. She handed Mrs. Calloway her prescription, smiled, and went back to work.

But she didn't forget it.

By afternoon, she'd heard it from three more patients. All different stories. One said he'd been found lying in a field, unconscious. Another said his horse had come back without him. A third said he'd been taken. Taken. Not lost. Taken.

Amelia didn't say anything. But she listened. And she remembered.

That night, she sat at her kitchen table. Luna slept on the windowsill. The forest was quiet. The pull in her chest was there again, faint but persistent, like a clock ticking in another room. Her hands were tingling. She told herself it was nothing.

It wasn't nothing.

---

Victor

Victor rode the northern boundary at dusk.

He was alone. It was against his orders, he'd told the others not to go alone, but he was the Alpha, and the Alpha didn't follow his own rules when it mattered. He was the best tracker in the pack. He was the one who could find a scent in a storm, who could read a trail like a book, who could smell fear on a man three miles away.

He needed to find Marcus.

He followed the scent trail from the supply room clipboard to the last marker. Marcus's scent was fresh, too fresh. It led north, through the trees, through the underbrush, and then it ended. Just like that. No trace. No disturbance. Just silence.

Victor dismounted. He ran his hands over the ground. No drag marks. No blood. No signs of a struggle. He closed his eyes. He breathed in. The scent trail just stopped. It didn't fade. It didn't trail off. It just ended, like a thread cut cleanly with a pair of scissors.

He didn't like it.

He didn't like the way the forest looked around him, the trees, tall and dark, their branches weaving together like hands reaching over his head. The way the fog was starting to roll in, low and gray, creeping between the trunks like something alive. The way the silence felt heavy, like the whole forest was holding its breath, waiting for something to happen.

He didn't like it.

But he also felt it, the pull in his chest, sharp and insistent, pointing north, toward whatever had taken Marcus, toward whatever was waiting in the dark.

He stood there for a long time. He thought about going further. He thought about following the pull, following whatever it was that was pulling at him like a thread, into the dark, into the fog, into whatever was waiting.

He didn't go.

He got back on his horse. He rode back to the compound. And he told himself he was fine.

It wasn't fine.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 7: The Forest

Amelia

Wrong in the way silence changes, when the birds don't sing where they should, when the air smells like nothing at all.

Amelia knew the woods. She'd spent her whole life near them, walking through them, gathering herbs, knowing every path and clearing and the way light fell through the canopy. She knew what a healthy forest sounded like.

This wasn't healthy.

She'd been thinking about it for days. Trying to put her finger on something she couldn't quite name. She told herself it was nothing. It was the season. It was the weather. It was nerves from the rumors people were spreading.

But her hands were still tingling.

Every morning when she woke up, her fingers felt like they'd fallen asleep, and every night when she lay down, her chest ached with that same pulling sensation, steady, insistent, pointing north, toward the tree line, toward the boundary of the land she knew and the land she didn't.

She told herself it was curiosity.

It wasn't curiosity.

---

The rumors had started after the missing hunter. That much she knew, or at least, that much she'd heard people talking about at the market, at the general store, at the post office. People were nervous. They talked in hushed tones when they thought she wasn't listening. They talked louder when they thought she was.

One woman said there had been lights in the woods, strange, flickering lights that moved in patterns that didn't make sense. Another said a farmer's dog had gone out one morning and come back the next, trembling, with its fur covered in frost even though the weather had been warm. A third said she'd seen something in the trees, something tall and dark that wasn't there when she looked again.

Amelia didn't believe in ghosts. She didn't believe in monsters. But she was a healer, and healers knew what real injuries looked like. She'd seen enough strange wounds in her time to know that not everything that hurt you came from a tree branch or a fall or a fever.

Some things came from somewhere else entirely.

That night, she sat at her kitchen table and made a decision.

She would go to the boundary. She would investigate. She would find out what was going on.

She wasn't a foolish woman. She knew the risks. She packed supplies, dried food, water, a lantern, matches, a knife, bandages, herbs. She told Luna to stay home. She locked the cottage door and walked north.

The tingling in her hands got stronger the further she went.

By the time she reached the edge of the tree line, her fingers were vibrating. She stopped. She looked at them, pale, ordinary, scarred from a thousand small cuts and burns. She didn't understand why they were tingling. She didn't understand why her chest felt tight. Didn't understand why the pull in her bones felt like something reaching for her, like a hand stretching across the dark.

She told herself it was the cold.

It wasn't the cold.

She walked into the forest.

---

The deeper she went, the stranger it got.

The air felt thick — not humid, not heavy with rain, but dense, like standing too close to something humming. A low vibration she could feel in her skin but not hear.

She couldn't explain it.

The trees were tall, older than she'd ever seen them. Their trunks wider, their branches higher, and their leaves a darker green than anything should have been. The forest smelled different here, too. Not like pine or damp earth or the sweet decay of fallen leaves. It smelled like something else entirely, something metallic, sharp, almost electric, like the air before a storm.

She didn't understand it. But she kept walking.

By evening, the light had changed. The canopy had closed overhead, and the forest floor was dim, even though the sky was clear. Shadows moved between the trees, not just the ordinary shadows of branches swaying in the wind, but wrong, like they were flickering in and out of sight, like they were stepping through something she couldn't see.

She turned toward them.

They stopped.

She couldn't see them. Not really. She could see their outlines, dark shapes against the darker background of the forest, but when she tried to focus on them, they vanished. Just like that. Gone. As if they'd never been there at all.

But she could feel them.

She could feel them looking at her.

It wasn't fear. Not exactly. It was worse than fear, it was the knowledge that something was watching her, something that existed in a space she couldn't perceive, something that was aware of her presence and was studying her in return. She could feel it the way you feel someone looking at your back when you're walking down a dark street. You can't see them. You know they're there. And that knowledge sits in your chest like a stone.

She stood very still.

The shadows didn't move.

She didn't either.

After a long time, she turned around and walked away. She didn't run. She didn't look back. She just kept walking, faster and faster, until she reached the edge of the tree line and could see the sky again.

She stopped. She breathed. She turned around and looked at the forest.

It looked normal. Just trees. Just shadows. Just quiet.

She didn't know what she'd seen. She didn't know what she'd felt. But she knew, with a certainty that sat deep in her bones, that something was out there. Something on the other side of something she couldn't name.

She didn't go back that night.

She set up camp a short distance from the boundary, close enough to see the tree line, far enough that the shadows didn't follow her. She built a small fire, ate a piece of dried bread, then sat on her pack and listened to the forest and waited for the sun to go down.

The tingling in her hands didn't stop.

It never stopped.

---

That night, she dreamed of the forest.

Not the same forest, not the one she'd walked through during the day. This forest was darker, deeper, filled with trees that stretched up into nothing, their branches twisting and turning like hands reaching for something they'd never touch. The air was thick with mist, gray, cold, alive.

And in the mist, she saw them.

Shadows. Tall, dark, shapeless shadows that moved through the trees like smoke, flickering in and out of sight. They weren't real. She knew they weren't real. But they were watching her. They were looking at her with something that might have been hunger, might have been curiosity, might have been something she didn't have a name for.

She woke.

Her hands were tingling.

The fire was burning low.

The forest was quiet.

And somewhere in the dark, beyond the tree line, something was watching her.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 8: The Encounter

Amelia woke before the sun.

She slept on her pack, not the most comfortable bed she'd ever had, but it would do. The fire had burned down to embers and the forest was still dark, and the sky still black, the air still cold. She sat up, rubbed her face, and looked at her hands.

They were tingling.

They had been tingling all night. Not strong, not overwhelming — just there, a low vibration like a phone on silent in the next room. She had woken up three times in the night, half-asleep, trying to figure out what was making that noise. It hadn't been the fire. It hadn't been the wind. It had been her hands.

She got up. She brushed off her clothes. She packed the fire and put out the embers with dirt. She ate a piece of dried bread and drank from her water skin. And then she walked into the forest.

---

The forest was different at dawn.

The mist had risen from the ground, low and gray, curling between the trees like smoke. The air was damp, cool, smelling of pine and damp earth and the faint metallic tang of something she couldn't name. The birds had started singing, a few of them, cautious, testing the morning like it was something fragile.

She followed the sound of water.

She hadn't planned to go far. She had planned to walk a short distance, look around, take notes, and come back. But the deeper she went, the more the forest opened up. The trees thinned, the canopy broke, and she could see a stream ahead, narrow and shallow, winding through the rocks and moss like a silver ribbon.

She sat down beside it. She pulled out her water skin, filled it, and drank.

And then she heard the snapping of a branch.

She looked up.

He was standing at the edge of the clearing, tall, dark-haired, gray eyes the color of storm clouds, his body built like someone who had spent his entire life training for a war he didn't know was coming. He was wearing a dark jacket, jeans, and boots. He looked like someone who belonged in the forest.

And he was looking at her.

The bond detonated.

She felt it like a physical blow — a surge of electricity that started in her hands and shot up her arms, through her shoulders, into her chest, like gravity had shifted and pulled everything toward him. Her water skin slipped from her fingers and hit the ground. Her hands were vibrating so hard she couldn't feel them. Her heart was hammering against her ribs.

He froze.

She saw it happen — the way his body went rigid, the way his eyes widened, the way his hands clenched at his sides. He looked like someone who had been hit by a truck. He looked like someone who had felt something he couldn't explain.

He was looking at her. She was looking at him. And neither of them knew what to do.

---

It was the longest three seconds of her life.

She could feel it, the pull, the tension, the invisible thread stretching between them, pulling her toward him with a force she had never experienced in her entire existence. It was physical, undeniable, and terrifying. It was like standing at the edge of a cliff and feeling the wind lift your feet off the ground.

He took a step toward her.

She didn't know why. She just knew he couldn't look away.

Then he spoke.

"You're not supposed to be out here," he said. His voice was low, commanding, but there was something in it that surprised even him — uncertainty, a crack in the armor, a moment of doubt that he couldn't hide.

She looked at him. She was measured. She was not cowering. She was not running. She was standing there, looking at this man who had appeared out of the mist like he'd fallen from the sky, and she was trying to figure out who he was.

"Neither are you," she said.

He blinked. He didn't expect that. He hadn't expected anyone to talk back to him like that. He was used to fear. He was used to respect. He was used to obedience. He was not used to being told that he wasn't supposed to be somewhere, not by a woman who looked like she had stepped out of the forest itself, with her hair messy and her clothes dirty and her eyes wide and curious and not afraid at all.

He noticed all of that. He noticed the way her hands were shaking. He noticed the way her chest was rising and falling too fast. He noticed the way she looked at him, not with fear, not with awe, but with something that might have been recognition.

He noticed that she was the most beautiful woman he had ever seen.

He didn't want to notice that. He had other things to worry about. He had a missing hunter to find. He had a pack to protect. He had a boundary to guard.

But he noticed.

---

"What are you doing out here?" he asked. His voice was steadier now. He had pulled himself together. He was the Alpha. He was used to being in control.

"I live near here," she said. "I'm a healer. I was gathering water."

He looked at her. He really looked at her. He was a man who had spent his entire life reading people, their intentions, their fears, their tells. And he read something in her that unsettled him.

She was not afraid.

Not of him. Not of the forest. Not of anything.

He didn't know what to make of that.

"Your hands," he said. "They're shaking."

She looked at her hands. They were vibrating. She had noticed, of course she had noticed, but she hadn't said anything. She didn't want to seem strange. She didn't want to seem afraid.

"It's nothing," she said.

"It's not nothing," he said. He took another step toward her. He couldn't help it. The pull was too strong. The connection was too real. It was like gravity, like magnetism, like the moon pulling the tide. He didn't understand it. But he couldn't fight it.

She looked at him. Her eyes were dark, intelligent, alive. Her hair was messy, her clothes were dirty, her face was smudged with dirt. She was the most beautiful thing he had ever seen.

And she was looking at him like she had been waiting for him.

He didn't know why that thought had crossed his mind. He didn't know why it felt so true.

"Who are you?" he asked.

She didn't answer.

He didn't know if she had forgotten her name, or if she was just deciding whether to trust him, or if she was trying to figure out who he was in return. He waited. He didn't move. He didn't breathe. He just stood there, in the mist, in the forest, in the space between the trees, and he waited for her to speak.

"Amelia," she said.

"Victor," he said.

Her name was Amelia. His name was Victor. And for a moment, just a moment, just a heartbeat, the world felt complete.

Then the mist thickened. The forest went quiet. And neither of them knew what to do next.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 9: The Return

Victor

The mist thickened. The forest went quiet. And neither of them knew what to do next.

Then he blinked. He shook his head. He didn't know why, but the feeling between them was still there, the pull, the tension, the invisible thread stretching from her chest to his. He couldn't let her walk back through the forest alone. He couldn't explain why he knew that. He just did.

"Amelia," he said. He didn't know why he said her name again. It felt right in his mouth. "You can't stay out here."

She looked at him. She didn't answer right away. She was looking at him like she was trying to figure him out the same way he was trying to figure her out.

"Why not?" she asked.

"Because you're a human woman in a forest that's not safe." He didn't know why he was saying that. It wasn't entirely true. The forest wasn't safe for her. It was also not safe for anyone else. But he didn't know how to say that. He didn't know how to say any of it.

She was quiet. Then she said, "I live near here."

"I heard you."

"Then you know I can take care of myself."

He wanted to tell her she was right, that she was the strongest person he'd ever met in his life. But, he didn't.

"I know you can," he said instead. "But I'm not asking you to go back alone."

She studied him. He studied her back. The bond between them hummed — low, steady, like a heartbeat. She didn't say anything for a long moment. Then she said, "What are you saying?"

"I'm saying you're coming with me."

"To where?"

"To the compound. It's safe. There are — there are people there. Guards. You'll be fine."

She thought about it. He could see her thinking, the way her eyes narrowed slightly, the way her mouth pressed into a thin line, the way her hands flexed at her sides. She was weighing him. Deciding whether to trust him. The weight of that decision sat on his chest like a stone.

"Okay," she said.

He didn't expect that. "Okay?"

"Yes. I'm coming with you. But I'm not — I'm not your prisoner. I'm not doing anything you're making me do. I'm deciding this. Do you understand?"

He nodded. He understood perfectly. "Yes. You're deciding this."

"Good."

He turned. He walked toward his horse. He didn't look back. He didn't need to. He could feel her behind him, the bond pulling him, the thread stretching between them, warm and bright and undeniable.

He reached the horse, and untied it from the tree, then turned to her.

"Get on," he said.

He held out his hand. His palm was sweating. He hated that his palm was sweating.

She looked at his hand. She looked at him. She looked at the horse. And then, without a word, she took his hand.

His fingers closed around hers.

The bond flared, sharp, bright, immediate, and for a second he saw stars behind his eyes. He gritted his teeth. She gasped. Then she pulled her hand back like she'd been burned.

"Sorry," he said. "I forget, human strength. I mean, I wasn't —"

He stopped. He didn't know what he was going to say. He didn't know how to explain a sensation he couldn't name to a woman who didn't know he could name it.

"It's fine," she said. "I'm fine. I can —" She gestured at the horse. "I can get up. I'm not made of glass."

He was quiet. He wanted to tell her she wasn't. He wanted to tell her she was something else entirely. He didn't.

"Let me help," he said instead.

He stepped closer. He reached for her waist, then stopped. His hand hovered. He didn't know if he should. He didn't know if she would let him.

She looked at his hand. She looked at his face. And she nodded.

His fingers closed around her waist. She was smaller than he expected. Smaller than he wanted. His hand nearly reached all the way across her. He felt the heat of her through her clothes, the softness of her, the way her breath caught when he pulled her up.

She was standing in front of him now, her hands on his shoulders for balance, her eyes wide and startled and trusting in a way that made his chest ache. He let go of her waist. She stepped back. The bond dimmed.

He swung his leg over the saddle and mounted. Then he reached down and steadied her as she climbed up behind him. Her back pressed to his chest. Her hands gripped his jacket. For one second, just one second, they were pressed together, and the bond went so bright he thought he might actually see it.

---

Amelia

The ride back was the longest three miles of her life.

She sat behind Victor on the horse, her hands gripping his jacket, her body pressed close to his back, and every inch of her skin was alive. The bond was there, not as strong as it had been when they first met, not the detonation she'd felt at the stream, but there, a low hum in the back of her skull like a phone on silent, like a voice she couldn't hear but could feel. She tried to ignore it. She couldn't.

She tried to think about anything else. She tried to think about her clinic. She tried to think about the herbs she needed to gather. She tried to think about Luna waiting at home. But she couldn't stop thinking about the man in front of her, his shoulders, solid and warm under her hands, the way his muscles moved when the horse walked, the way his breathing changed when she shifted behind him.

She didn't know what this was. She didn't know what he was. She didn't know anything.

But she knew one thing: she didn't want to be away from him.

And that scared her more than the forest had.

---

They reached the compound as the sun was setting.

Amelia saw it from a distance, not a building, exactly, but a complex. Multiple structures connected by wooden walkways, surrounded by high fences and trees. It looked like something from a movie, not real life. It looked like a place built for people who could run faster than humans, stronger than humans, see further than humans.

She didn't say any of that. She just sat on the horse and watched.

They passed through a gate. Guards, armed, alert, clearly trained, stopped them. Victor spoke to them in a voice that was different from the voice he'd used with her. Calmer. More authoritative. More in control. The guards nodded and stepped aside.

Inside, the compound was alive. People were everywhere. Men and women moving with purpose, some carrying tools, some carrying weapons, and some carrying nothing but urgency. They all looked up when Victor and Amelia entered. Some of them stared, some of them whispered, and some looked at her with open hostility.

Amelia kept her head up. She wasn't cowering, she wasn't running. She was sitting on a horse in front of a hundred people who could probably kill her with their bare hands, and she was trying to figure out who she was in this place and why she was here and what was going to happen next.

Victor dismounted first. Then he turned and offered his hand.

She took it. His fingers closed around hers for a second. The bond flared, bright, sudden, undeniable, and she felt it, felt him, felt the way his body tensed when she touched him, the way his breath caught, the way his eyes flicked to her mouth for half a second before he looked away.

Then she let go. She got off the horse, stood in front of him, and she waited.

He took her hand again and led her inside.

---

Victor

He walked her to the main hall. He didn't let go of her hand until they were inside. He didn't know why. He knew why. He just didn't want to admit it.

The hall was large, stone floor, high ceiling, fireplaces on either end. There were people sitting around long tables, eating dinner. They looked up when he entered. The room went quiet. Victor didn't care. He was focused on Amelia, on the way she looked around, on the way her eyes moved from face to face, on the way her body was tense but her posture was straight.

She was scared. He could feel it through the bond, not panic, not terror, but caution, wariness, the careful assessment of someone who didn't know what to trust yet. He felt the need to protect her. It was a physical sensation, like a weight on his chest, like a hand reaching out and squeezing his heart. He hated it. He wanted it. He didn't know what to do with either.

"Perseus," he said.

Perseus was across the room. He was sitting at the table, talking to someone, looking at a map, and the moment Victor entered, he was up. He came over. He looked at Victor. He looked at Amelia. He looked at Victor again.

"You brought a human," he said.

"I brought a witness," Victor said. "A human woman. She lives near the boundary. She saw —" He hesitated. He didn't know what to say. He didn't know how to explain that they hadn't seen anything yet. They'd just looked at each other. They'd just existed in the same space. But that was enough. "She saw something. I need you to question her."

Perseus's eyes narrowed. He looked at Amelia. He was studying her the way he studied everything, calculating, weighing, deciding whether someone was a threat or an asset. It was unsettling to be looked at like that. It was unsettling to be looked at by someone who could read people the way Victor could.

"Come with me," Perseus said to Amelia. "I have questions."

She nodded. She didn't hesitate. She didn't ask Victor for permission. She just looked at Perseus and said, "Sure."

And then she walked away.

Victor watched her go. He couldn't help it. The bond tugged at him, a thin, invisible thread stretching from his chest to hers, pulling him in the direction she moved. He gritted his teeth. He turned to David.

"David," Victor said. "Get over here."

David appeared like he'd been waiting for this. He was leaning against a pillar, arms crossed, grin on his face, and when he saw Amelia walking away with Perseus, his grin got wider.

"You brought home a stray?" David said. "Really, Victor? A stray?"

Victor's voice was flat. It was deadly. "She's not a stray."

David's grin didn't fade. He just shrugged. "I said he brought home a stray. There's a difference."

"You'll watch your mouth," Victor said. "Around her. Around everyone."

David's smile faded. He nodded. "Yeah. Okay."

Victor turned away. He didn't want to deal with David. He didn't want to deal with anything. He wanted to sit down, close his eyes, and think about what had happened today and what it meant and what Josiah was going to tell him when he had a chance to speak to him.

But Josiah was already on his way.

Josiah was moving slowly across the room, leaning on his staff, and when he got to Victor, he didn't look at him. He looked past him. He was watching Amelia. He was watching her talk to Perseus, watching the way Perseus's face was unreadable, watching the way Amelia was standing straight and speaking clearly. Josiah's eyes were old and knowing and unreadable.

"You know her," Josiah said. It wasn't a question.

"No," Victor said. "I don't."

"You do," Josiah said. He turned to Victor and looked at him. His eyes were heavy. His voice was low. "You just didn't know you knew her."

Victor didn't answer. He couldn't. Because Josiah was right.

---

Amelia

Perseus's office was small. Stone walls, wooden desk, a single window looking out over the compound. There were books everywhere, stacks of them on shelves, on the floor, on the desk. She noticed all of that. She noticed the way Perseus closed the door behind them. She noticed the way he sat down and gestured for her to sit.

"Tell me what you saw," Perseus said.

"I saw a man," she said. "Tall, dark hair, gray eyes. He was — " She stopped. She couldn't explain what he was or what he was doing out there at that hour. He had moved through the trees like he owned the shadows, and when he looked at her, it was like he was looking through her skin. She tried to describe it to Perseus, but the words kept slipping away.

"Where?" Perseus said.

"By a stream. Near the northern boundary. He was patrolling. He was — alone."

Perseus was quiet. He was looking at her. He was waiting.

"Did you feel anything unusual?" he asked.

She thought about it. She thought about the tingling in her hands, the pull in her chest, the way her body had reacted to him like it had been waiting for him. She thought about the way the forest had felt different when he was near, not dangerous, just… charged.

"Yes," she said.

"What?"

"I don't know. A sensation. In my hands. In my chest."

Perseus nodded. He wrote something down. He was writing fast, his handwriting tight and precise. He didn't look up.

"How old are you?" he asked.

"Twenty-six."

"Where do you live?"

"Outside the boundary. Near the tree line. I have a cottage. I'm a healer."

He looked up. "A healer. Of humans?"

"Of anyone who needs it. Mostly humans, but — sometimes people come to me in the middle of the night. They don't talk about it, but I treat them anyway."

Perseus was quiet for a long moment. Then he said, "You've treated people who shouldn't be able to survive those kinds of injuries before?"

"Yes."

"How?"

"I don't know. They come to my clinic. They say they fell. Or they got stuck in a thorn bush. Or they were attacked by a dog. But the injuries don't match. So I treat them anyway."

Perseus set his pen down. He looked at her. Really looked at her.

"You're a useful person," he said. "You know what that means?"

"It means I'm not useless."

"It means you might be more important than you realize."

She didn't know what to say to that. She just nodded.

Perseus stood up. "You can go, Victor will tell you what to do next."

She stood up too. She was about to leave when Perseus spoke again.

"One more thing," he said. "When you're with him — when you're near him — do you feel anything else? Anything that doesn't make sense?"

She hesitated. She thought about the tingling. She thought about the pull. She thought about the way her heart had skipped when he touched her waist, the way her body had reacted before her mind even knew what was happening.

"Yes," she said.

"What?"

"I don't know. It's — it's like something inside me woke up. And I don't know what it's waking up to."

Perseus was quiet. He looked at her for a long moment. Then he opened the door and let her out.

---

Amelia

She walked out of the office into the hallway. The compound was alive around her, voices echoing off the stone walls, footsteps, the smell of food, the sound of laughter. It was overwhelming. She didn't know what to do with herself.

She stood in the hallway for a moment. She didn't know where to go. She didn't know who to ask. She didn't know anything.

And then she heard his voice.

"I don't know, Josiah. I really don't."

It was Victor. She didn't know how she knew it was Victor, but she did. The bond pulled her toward it, a thread stretching through the walls, through the air, through the noise of the compound, pulling her toward the sound of his voice.

She followed it.

She found him in a small room near the back of the compound. It was quiet. There was a fireplace. There was a chair. There was Josiah. And there was Victor, standing in front of the fire, his back to her, his hands clenched at his sides.

Josiah turned when she entered. He looked at her. He looked at him. Then, he looked at the two of them together, and he smiled.

"Amelia," he said. "Come in. Sit down."

She didn't know if she should. She looked at Victor. He was still facing the fire. He hadn't turned around. But she could feel him, the way his body tensed when she entered, the way his breathing changed, the way the bond between them flared like a struck match.

She walked in. She sat down.

Josiah looked at Victor. "You know," he said. "We need to talk about this. The bond, the Mate Bond, the Moonlit Rite, and the Moon Goddess. All of it."

Victor didn't answer.

Josiah looked at Amelia. "Amelia," he said. "Do you know what a Mate Bond is?"

She shook her head.

He sat down and leaned forward. His hands were resting on his staff. His eyes were gentle. His voice was patient.

"Let me tell you a story," he said.

And he began.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 10: The Elder's Knowing

Josiah told them a story about the Moon Goddess.

He told them about the shifters, how they had split apart centuries ago, how the different types had forgotten each other, how the Veil between them had grown thick with time and fear.
