The knife rested at Victor's belt. Marcus's hand reached again, fingers rigid, and the empty space where his friend should have been opened inside his chest.

Victor moved inside the next strike and drove the knife beneath Marcus's jaw.

The body went slack.

Victor caught him on the way down. For half a second, he held Marcus against his chest as if that could change what had happened. Then he lowered him to the ground.

Marcus's chest rose once.

Then again.

The patrolman made a small, broken sound behind them.

"Get him back to the compound," Victor said. His hand was slick around the knife. "Keep him restrained. No one approaches alone."

David pushed himself up, one hand pressed to his shoulder. "He's still breathing."

"I can see that."

The words came out too sharply. Victor looked down at Marcus. His eyes remained open. Whatever had made him Marcus was gone, but his body had not understood.

"It's the Hollow King," Samuel said. His voice was low. "He sent him back."

Victor wiped the knife on the wet grass and sheathed it. "Then the Hollow King can come answer for it."

No one argued. They all knew Victor was speaking to the trees because there was no one else there to hear him.

---

Amelia

Emila was gathering the cups when Amelia's hands began to tingle again.

The sensation sharpened, spreading into her palms. She caught the edge of the table. The cups rattled. One tipped, spilling tea across the wood.

Emila turned at once. "Amelia?"

She flexed her fingers. The bond carried no pain from Victor, no thoughts, no image. Only the familiar pull north, toward the compound.

"Victor," she said.

Emila's expression changed. She was already reaching for her coat. "We should go."

Two riders came through the gate, one leading a horse with a man bound across the saddle. A third brought the injured patrolman in behind them. Victor walked beside the bound horse, his jacket dark with rain. There was blood on one hand.

Amelia went straight to him. "Are you hurt?"

"No."

She looked at the blood. "Is it yours?"

"No."

He gave no more explanation. The blood on his hand and the stillness in his face told her not to press.

The bound man was taken toward the healing tent. A healer guided the injured patrolman after him, one hand firm at his elbow. David followed, favoring one arm. Samuel stayed by the gate, giving orders to the guards. Everyone moved as if the compound had become a place where sudden sounds could mean danger.

Amelia kept her eyes on Victor. "Who was it?"

"Marcus LaBlanc."

She remembered the name from the search, from the weeks of questions and the empty spaces in every answer. "He was missing."

"He came back." Victor's gaze dropped to his hand. "Not as himself."

Amelia looked toward the healing tent. "Can he hear us?"

"I called his name."

It wasn't an answer. She understood anyway. Her training had taught her how to find breath, a pulse, a response to pain. It hadn't prepared her to look at a living body and wonder if the person inside it was gone.

"I can help Emila check him," she said.

"Not until we know the restraints will hold." His voice was firm, but his eyes stayed on the tent. "Stay with her. Don't go in alone."

The words were an order. The tremor in his fingers made them something else.

"The Hollow King did this?"

"Yes."

Amelia wanted to say something that would make it easier. Nothing came. She had seen grief before. She knew how useless it was to dress it up as comfort.

"I can stay," she said.

His eyes lifted to hers. He looked tired in a way sleep would not fix. "You don't have to."

"I know."

She held out her hand, palm up, and let him decide.

For a moment he only stared at it. Then he set his bloodstained hand in hers, careful not to smear it across her skin. His palm was warm. His pulse beat fast beneath her fingers.

Under their joined hands, the thread drew taut, then settled, warmer than before. It didn't open, and it gave her no thoughts or clear picture of what he had felt in the clearing. His hand stayed in hers. That was enough to tell her he was still here.

Victor closed his eyes once, briefly. When he opened them, his fingers tightened around hers.

"I knew him for years," he said.

"I know."

"I had to stop him."

Amelia didn't tell him it was all right. It wasn't. She stayed beside him and held on.

From somewhere beyond the northern wall, the alarm bell rang.

Once.

Every conversation in the courtyard stopped. Samuel turned toward the tree line. The bound figure in the healing tent began to struggle against his restraints.

Then the bell rang again.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 16: The Pack Reacts

Victor

The second ring of the bell shattered everything.

Every conversation in the courtyard stopped. Boots hit gravel. Doors slammed open. People poured out of the halls like water breaking a dam, running toward the center, toward the healing tent, toward whatever sound had just split the morning in two.

Victor moved first. He crossed the compound in three long strides, his boots heavy on the packed earth, pulling Amelia along beside him, and reached the healing tent before most of the pack had even stopped running.

Samuel stood at the tent entrance, arms crossed, jaw clenched, watching with the kind of cold focus that came from years of patrol. He did not approach. He did not need to. His body said *stay back* with the same authority Victor used for pack decrees.

"Get out," Victor said to the crowd that had gathered at the tent edge. "Everyone out. Now."

No one moved at first. The pack had heard the bell. They had seen Marcus being brought in bound and breathing and *wrong*. Now they were seeing him thrashing and making that sound, and something primal had woken in their blood. Fear was not a shifter trait. It was the one trait that made them human. And it was spreading.

"David," Victor said.

David was already moving, his good arm out, pushing people back, herding them toward the compound center. "Move. Everyone to the meeting ground. Now."

Victor ducked under the tent flap and went inside. Amelia followed close behind.

"Victor." Amelia's voice was steady. "The restraints are holding. But he is not going to stop. Not on his own."

"He knows Marcus," Victor said. "Or he knew him. His body does not."

"I know." Amelia's fingers tightened on the floor. "His body is moving. But the person inside is gone. All I can find is breath and pulse and no one home."

Victor felt the thread between them tighten, warmth, awareness, the quiet hum of a connection that was still a thread. He could feel her steadiness. It grounded him. He stepped closer, stood behind her, let his hand rest on her shoulder. She leaned back into him, just for a second, just enough.

Then the sound stopped.

The bound figure went still. The grinding noise cut out. His body went limp. His eyes stayed open, staring at nothing.

The silence that followed was worse than the noise.

Amelia let out a breath she had been holding. "He's done," she said.

"Get him released," Victor told Samuel outside. "Put him in the east cell. Double restraints. No one approaches alone."

Samuel nodded once, already moving to fetch the key.

"Alpha?" One of the younger warriors stepped forward. A boy, really. Early twenties, lean, still filling out his frame. Grayden. "What do we do now? We can't keep —" He gestured at the tent. At the bound figure. At the hollowed.

"We do what we have always done," Victor said. "We hold the line. We do not hide. We do not retreat behind wards that were never meant to keep the world out. The pack stands together, or the pack does not stand at all. Clear?"

A murmur moved through the crowd. Some heads nodded. Some did not.

Perseus stepped into the clearing. His face was unreadable, his posture relaxed, but his eyes were sharp. He looked at the tent, at the crowd, at Victor, and he did not miss anything.

"Victor," he said, low enough that only Victor heard. "The war room. Now."

Victor looked at Amelia. She was still sitting on the floor. Her hands were steady. Her face was calm. But he could feel the tremor in her pulse, not fear. Empathy. She felt what the pack was feeling. The raw, unfiltered fear of a group of people who had just seen something that broke every rule of how the world was supposed to work.

"Stay here," Victor said.

Amelia shook her head. "I'm coming."

---

The war room was a long table with a territory map spread across it, shelves of supplies on the walls, and windows looking out over the northern boundary. Victor sat at the head. Perseus took the chair to his right. David stood at the window, arms crossed, staring north. Josiah sat in the corner chair, still and quiet, watching everything.

The pack filed in behind them, twenty-seven members, all the adults, all the warriors, all the Omegas who had opinions worth hearing. They took seats around the table or stood along the walls. The mood was tense. Not hostile. Not yet. But the kind of tension that preceded either courage or panic, and nobody knew which way it would break.

Amelia took a seat at the far end of the table, next to Emila, who pulled her chair closer without a word. Josiah caught Amelia's eye and nodded, small, deliberate, the kind of nod that said *you belong here* without speaking it aloud. Amelia felt something shift in her chest, not the bond, just the quiet certainty that she was being seen.

Victor stood at the head of the table. He did not need to raise his voice. He did not need to command attention. The room went quiet on its own.

"The hollowed warrior we found this morning," Victor began. "Marcus LaBlanc. He was missing. He is back. Not as himself. Something or someone took him and brought him back empty. His mind, his memories, his emotions. All gone. His body is alive. His heart is beating. But he is not Marcus anymore."

Silence. Heavy. Uncomfortable.

David spoke first. "We need to hit harder. Now. Whatever is out there, we find it and we break it before it breaks us."

Perseus did not look at him. He looked at Victor. "We need information. Before we make moves. We do not know what we are facing. We do not know how many hollowed there are. We do not know if Marcus was the first."

Josiah spoke quietly. "He was not. The old records say the Hollow King does not strike in one place at one time. He spreads. He tests. He waits to see where the pack is weakest. And he finds it."

The room shifted. Some faces tightened. Some looked down. Fear was no longer abstract. It had a face. It had a name. Marcus.

Victor looked at David. "I hear you. I want him gone as much as you do. But we do not charge blind. Not anymore."

David's jaw clenched. He wanted to argue. He wanted to run at the sky and tear it apart. But he held back. For now.

Perseus leaned forward. "Victor. What if this is not just a wolf territory problem? What if the Hollow King is targeting all shifter types? We know he is. We have records. The patterns match. Every time a bond forms, he strikes. And this time, the bond has just formed. We are the first test."

Victor was quiet. He was looking at the territory map. At the lines that divided pack boundaries. At the gaps between territories. At the places where the Veil was thin.

"Then we investigate," he said at last. "Not just the northern boundary. Every boundary. Every territory. We send teams to check the other packs. Panthers. Bears. Foxes. Eagles. Dragons. We find out if this is happening to all of them or just us."

Perseus nodded once. "I will organize teams. Two per territory. We leave within three days."

David spoke again, quieter this time. "What if the Hollow King follows them?"

"Then we make sure they are strong enough to handle it," Victor said.

A young warrior at the far end of the table, Grayden, David's mirror image, young and eager, spoke up. "Alpha — what about the wards? We should strengthen the wards. Make them impenetrable. Nothing gets in."

David shot him a look. "We are not a fortress. We are a pack."

"We should be both," Grayden shot back.

Victor raised a hand. The room went quiet again.

"The wards hold the Hollow King at bay. That is what they are for. We will reinforce them. But we do not hide behind them. If we spend all our energy building walls, we forget that we have weapons. And we are not defenseless."

The room settled. Not perfectly. Not with unanimous agreement. But with enough understanding for now.

Josiah leaned forward. "Victor has spoken. The pack does not hide. The pack does not retreat. We investigate. We prepare. We hold the line. And we do not forget that the Hollow King feeds on fear. Fear makes him stronger. Unity makes us stronger. Do not let him win that battle before the first arrow is shot."

The pack nodded. Some louder than others. But it was enough.

Victor nodded back once. "Dismissed." He turned and left the war room, Perseus falling into step beside him as the pack began to break.

---

After the meeting, the compound broke into its normal rhythm: patrols, training, meals, the steady hum of a community that refused to stop living just because something dark was circling.

Amelia walked home through the compound. She moved past the training yard where David was pushing warriors through combat drills, past the kitchen where Marco was arguing with the cook about spices, past the healing tent where Emila was setting up supplies for the evening. Everyone was busy. Everyone was doing their job. And under it all, she could feel the fear, quiet, held in, not spoken aloud, but present like a low-voltage current running through the pack's veins.

She had spent her entire life watching people. It was what healers did. You observed before you acted. You measured before you touched. And the pack was no different from a patient: you could see the tension in their shoulders, the tightness around their eyes, the way some people moved too fast while others moved too slow. Fear made people unpredictable. That was the dangerous part.

Josiah passed her in the path between the archive and the compound center. He did not slow down, but he did glance at her, just a flicker of his eyes, just a nod, just a *I see you*.

She stopped. "I was just watching."

Josiah did not look surprised. "You are a healer. Observation is your craft."

"I'm trying to understand."

Josiah smiled faintly. "Understanding is its own craft. You are doing well."

He moved on. She stood there for a moment, processing what he had said. *You are doing well.* That was not something she expected to hear from an Elder who had lived through centuries of pack politics and supernatural warfare and mate bonds and hollowed warriors. But it was enough to steady her.

She kept walking.

The compound gave way to the path that led to the edge of the territory, past the boundary stones, past the tree line, past the place where the forest opened into the meadow and the meadow gave way to the road and the road went to town. The air changed as she moved north. The Veil was thinner here. She could feel it, not like a wall or a barrier, exactly. More like standing too close to something massive and alive, like the air itself was vibrating. Cold water against her skin. A pressure on her chest that had nothing to do with the bond.

She looked up.

The sky looked wrong for a moment.

Not cloudy. Not storming. Just... wrong. The sky was shifting, like someone had rearranged the stars while she was not looking. The angles were off. The spacing was wrong. It lasted maybe three seconds. Then it passed, and the sky looked normal again.

Her hands started tingling.

Not from the Veil. From him.

Victor.

The pull was strong. Not the desperate, pulling sensation from before. More like a steady hum, a current running under her skin, saying *he is there, he is safe, he is thinking about you*. She could not read his thoughts, the thread was not open, but she could feel the shape of his presence, familiar, grounding, like a note held in the air.

She stood there at the edge of the territory, hands tingling, Veil pressing against her skin, stars rearranging and then not, and she thought: *This is what the world looks like now. This is what I live in.*

She did not hate it.

She kept walking.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 17: The Invitation

Victor

The dream came without warning.

He did not sleep deeply after the pack meeting. His body was exhausted from the hollowed, from the hollowed warrior's grinding, from the hollowed eyes staring at nothing. But his mind would not rest. It kept circling the territory map, the gaps between boundaries, the thin places where the Veil had worn through. He could still feel Amelia's pulse under his fingers. Steady. Steady. Steady.

Then the room changed.

The fire in the hearth went dark. The walls of his quarters dissolved into silver mist. He was standing in a clearing he did not recognize, under a sky that had no stars but glowed with something like moonlight anyway. The light came from everywhere and nowhere. It did not cast shadows. It made them impossible.

A figure stood at the center.

She was not solid. She was translucent, like moonlight made human. Silver hair, luminous eyes shifting between colors, the kind of beauty that made his chest tighten without permission. He knew her. He did not know how he knew her, but he did. Josiah's voice in his head: *Aurela. The Moon Goddess.*

She looked at him with the kind of attention that made him feel seen in a way that had nothing to do with eyes.

"Victor," she said. Her voice was not loud. It was not soft. It was everywhere, like the sound of wind through ancient trees, like the ocean heard from a great distance. "You have met her."

He felt his jaw clench. "Amelia."

"You met her. You brought her to your pack. You felt what happens when two people recognize each other across a distance too vast to be coincidence." Aurela tilted her head. "You do not yet understand what is happening. I can see that in your eyes. You think it is love. You think it is romance. I tell you it is something older. Something necessary."

Victor crossed his arms. His boots felt solid beneath him. His breathing felt steady. But the mist around him pulsed with something that was not wind. "Explain."

"The Hollow King is not the threat you think he is. He is a symptom. The disease is isolation. The Hollow King feeds on it. When shifters stand alone, when they distrust each other, when they hoard their strength and refuse to extend their bonds beyond their own territory, the Hollow King grows. He grows fat on your fear. He grows strong on your silence."

The light shifted. The clearing changed. Victor saw images without looking: a wolf pack standing alone on a mountain ridge, surrounded by darkness that crept closer and closer. A panther pack in a swamp, watching the water rise. A bear pack in the mountains, their fire dying. A fox pack in the desert, their paths closing in. An eagle pack in the peaks, their vision narrowing. A dragon pack in the volcanic lands, their heat fading.

Each pack was strong on its own. Each was surrounded. Each would fall.

"I cannot fight him," Aurela said. "Not directly. The Veil keeps me from entering the physical world. I can only work through the Veil. The only tool I have left is the Mate Bond. Each bond creates a bridge between two people who have no reason to trust each other. Wolf and human. Panther and wolf. Bear and wolf. The bonds multiply. They create a web. A barrier. A coalition."

The images shifted again. Bonds connecting the packs like threads of light, forming a barrier that pushed the darkness back. The Hollow King recoiling. The barrier holding.

"The first bond is always the hardest," Aurela said. "Because it requires the Alpha to trust something he cannot control. Victor, you are a man who holds everything together with his own hands. You carry your father's legacy like a shield. But the shield is not enough. The old ways are not enough. The Hollow King knows every tradition you have. He does not know this."

She pointed at him. At his chest.

"The Hollow King will come for her. He will come for you. He will test the bond. He will try to make you doubt it. And you must not doubt. Not because I made it so, but because the bond is a choice, and you chose her. Not because of magic. You. That is what makes it strong. That is what makes it a weapon."

The light pulsed. Victor felt his heartbeat rise.

"Other bonds will follow," Aurela said. "Perseus. David. Grayden. Samuel. Raine. Each one will find their mate. Each one will create a bridge. Each one will weaken the Hollow King. You are the first Alpha in centuries who has the chance to unite your pack, not through fear or tradition, but through connection. Do not waste it."

The mist began to close in. Aurela's form dimmed.

"Victor," she said. Her voice was fading. "The Hollow King targets bonds because they create barriers. If you refuse the bond, the barrier weakens. If you choose the bond, the barrier holds. Do not let fear make your decision for you."

The dream broke.

---

Victor woke in his chair.

His head was on the map spread across his desk, his neck ached, his hands were cold. The first thing he thought was: *Aurela lies.*

The second thing he thought was: *I do not believe her. Not all of it.*

The third thing he thought was: *I have to see her. I need to see her face.*

He stood up so fast his chair scraped against the floor. He didn't pick up the scattered papers. He grabbed his jacket, threw it on, and walked out of his quarters. He walked the edge of the territory, past the boundary stones, and the bond hummed between him and Amelia like a wire pulled taut.

She was at her cottage. He stopped outside her door. He did not knock. He stood there, listening to the silence, feeling the hum in his chest grow stronger, and he thought: *She is right there. I could go in. I could talk to her. I could tell her what I saw. I could tell her what she means.*

But he did not knock. He did not go in. He turned and walked away.

He walked back through the trees to the compound. His quarters were cold. The fire in the hearth was dead. He sat on the edge of his bed, pressed his palms flat against his knees, and closed his eyes.

*The first bridge is always the hardest.*

*If you refuse the bond, the barrier weakens.*

*Do not let fear make your decision for you.*

"Shut up," he said. Not to the room. To her. To Aurela. To the voice that sounded like the ocean and the wind and something ancient and calculating.

"You do not get to tell me what to do."

He opened his eyes. The room was exactly as he had left it. No Aurela. No mist. No light. Just his quarters, his bed, the cold, and the bone-deep ache of a bond he had not asked for and could not deny.

He threw himself face-down onto the bed.

His hands were shaking.

He had faced warriors twice his size. He had taken blows that would have killed a man. He had shifted in combat with blood in his eyes and his pack's lives hanging on the outcome. But none of that shook him like a dream. None of that made his hands tremble like a goddess telling him his mate was not a love story but a weapon.

He pressed his face into the pillow. He breathed. He counted. He let his breathing slow.

He was Victor Slade. Alpha of the Blackwood Pack. He did not let fear control him. He did not let anyone control him. Not Aurela. Not the Hollow King. Not the Moonlit Rite.

But he also knew, with a certainty that terrified him, that he could not stop thinking about Amelia. He could not stop feeling the hum in his chest when he was apart from her. He could not stop seeing the way she looked at him when she said she wanted truth, not fantasy.

He rolled onto his back. He stared at the ceiling. He let his hands rest flat against his sides. He let his breathing steady.

In the morning, he would go to the war room. He would look at the map. He would make decisions for the pack. He would be the Alpha.

But right now, he was just a man who had been told by a goddess that his mate was a weapon, and he was not sure whether that made him angry or grateful or something he did not have a word for yet.

He closed his eyes. He did not sleep. He thought about her.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 18: The First Date (Sort Of)

Amelia

The kitchen at dawn smelled like burnt toast and bad decisions.

She couldn't sleep. The bond hummed in her chest, pulling her toward the compound, so she walked over, wrapped in a coat, and found herself in the kitchen at five. She had no excuse. No reason. Just a pull she couldn't ignore. Now it was morning, and the tea was cold and she was standing here, and the bond was humming louder than ever.

She stood at the counter with a kettle she didn't need to boil, a mug she didn't need to fill, and a cup of tea that had gone cold three minutes ago. The compound's kitchen was too clean, too organized, too much like a place someone who didn't cook came to make other people feel welcome. There were knives on a magnetic strip, perfectly aligned. Spices in labeled jars. A stand mixer she would probably never use.

The back door opened.

She didn't turn around right away. She listened to his footsteps, heavy and deliberate, but quieter than they would be during the day. He stopped three paces behind her. She could feel the bond pull, felt it like heat on the back of her neck.

"Morning," he said.

"Morning."

He was quiet for a long moment. She could feel him watching her. She could also feel that he was trying not to look like he was watching her, which made it worse somehow.

"Is that tea cold?" he asked.

She turned. He was wearing a dark t-shirt and a pair of jeans that had seen better days, his hair still sleep-tousled, his eyes the color of storm clouds and probably a dozen other things she was not supposed to be thinking about at six in the morning. He looked like a man who had not slept. She looked like a woman who had not slept. That should have been a joke. It was not a joke.

"Three minutes ago," she said.

He rubbed the back of his neck. "I come down here sometimes. Before the day starts. I don't know why. It's just quiet."

"I like quiet," she said.

He nodded. He leaned against the far counter, arms crossed, and she watched him do it, the way his shoulders relaxed when he wasn't giving orders, the way his jaw unclenched when he wasn't managing a pack. He looked different when no one was watching. Softer. Less guarded. It made something in her chest ache, and not because of the bond.

"You don't have to stay in the guest quarters," he said.

She blinked. The tea, the cold kitchen, the bad timing, none of it mattered. This was the wrong place to have this conversation. But she was not going to pretend it wasn't happening.

"I don't have to stay where?"

"In the guest quarters. There's a room. The last one was used for storage. It's clean. The bed is fine. You don't have to — I'm not asking you to move in. I'm just saying. If you wanted to. There's a room."

She stared at him. He was looking at the wall now, his jaw tight, like he was already regretting saying it out loud. He had offered her a room in his compound and made it sound like he was apologizing for it.

"That's very generous," she said. "But I need to think about Luna."

Victor's eyes went to hers immediately. Not the tea. Not the room. Luna.

"Your cat."

"She has a name. And she's not a pet. She's a cat." She set the mug down on the counter and turned to face him fully. "She stays where she's safe. If I move to the compound, she comes with me. If she doesn't come, I don't come."

Victor was quiet. He studied her face like he was trying to read something he couldn't quite see. Then he nodded.

"She comes," he said. "Of course she comes."

She waited. That was it? Of course she comes?

"Thank you," she said, and he looked surprised, like he hadn't expected gratitude. Or maybe he had, and he just didn't know what to do with it.

"It's just a room," he said.

"It's not just a room," she said. "But I'll think about it. Okay?"

He nodded again. "Okay."

They stood in the kitchen for a moment, neither of them saying anything, the bond humming between them like a second heartbeat. Then she spoke.

"What's it like?" she asked. "Being the Alpha?"

He looked at her, really looked at her, and something shifted. He wasn't the Alpha right now. He was just Victor.

"It's heavy," he said. "It's standing in a room full of people who depend on you for their safety and knowing that every decision you make could save them or kill them. It's feeling like the weight of the pack sits on your shoulders and you can't put it down because they need you to carry it. It's knowing that if you make the wrong call, people die. It's—"

He stopped. He took a breath. He looked at the wall again.

"My father built the Blackwood Pack into something strong. He kept them alive through three winters, through two wars, through everything. He died protecting them."

Amelia didn't speak. She just waited.

"I carry his legacy," he said. "I hold everything together with my own hands. Tradition. Strength. Order. But sometimes I look at the map and I can see the gaps. The thin places. And I know the old ways aren't enough anymore. I just don't know what is."

She set her hands flat on the counter. She was a healer. She knew how to measure before she touched. This was no different.

"Maybe the answer isn't a new way," she said. "Maybe it's just letting other people carry the weight for a while."

He looked at her. His eyes were gray, storm-cloud gray, unreadable. He looked like a man who hadn't expected someone to understand him.

"Amelia," he said.

She was close to him now. Not deliberately. She hadn't moved. He had, or she had, or the bond had pulled them together without either of them noticing. The space between them was small. The bond was loud. She could feel his presence like heat, like a note held in the air, like everything she had been waiting for without knowing it.

He didn't step back.

She didn't step back.

His eyes dropped to her mouth. Hers dropped to his.

The bond flared.

It was like a wire snapping taut, a current running through her veins, everything in her body reaching for him.

The back door banged open.

"Alpha — David said you'd be down here —" Marco stood in the doorway with a stack of supply reports in his arms, his eyes wide, his mouth open, and he looked at them both like he had just walked into something he was not supposed to see.

Victor stepped back so fast he nearly tripped over the counter. Amelia felt the bond break like a snapped wire and the loss hit her like a physical blow.

"Marco," Victor said, and his voice was steady, but it was too steady, like he was holding himself together by force.

"Sorry," Marco said. He looked at the stack of reports in his arms like they were suddenly very interesting. "Sorry. I'll come back later."

He backed out of the kitchen. The door closed. The silence that followed was worse than the interruption.

Amelia looked at Victor. Victor looked at the wall. The bond hummed between them, low and steady, like a promise neither of them was ready to keep.

The rest of the day passed in fragments. She went back to the compound. He went to war room duties. She told herself she was fine. She wasn't. She thought about his face when the door opened. She thought about the way he looked at the wall like it was the only thing keeping him from saying something he couldn't take back. She had told herself it was nothing. It was everything.

---

Evening came. The sky turned the color of bruised plums, and Amelia stood on the roof of the compound, watching the sun sink behind the ridge, and the bond between her and Victor hummed like a second heartbeat in her chest.

She had not meant to come here. She had meant to sit at her kitchen table and read her notes and pretend she was fine and think about a room in the compound and a cat who slept on her feet and a man who offered her a room like he was apologizing for it. But the bond pulled, and she followed it, and now she was sitting on the compound roof, waiting.

He arrived five minutes later.

He didn't knock. He didn't call out. He just climbed the stairs and walked onto the roof and sat down three feet from her, close enough that she could feel his warmth, far enough that she didn't have to look at him.

They sat in silence for a long time. The bond was steady between them, like a wire pulled taut but not broken.

Amelia closed her eyes. She let her hands rest on her knees. She let her breathing slow. She thought about why she trusted him. The way he'd said her cat's name without hesitation, like Luna was part of her and part of him, and he'd accept both without question. The way he'd looked at the wall when he talked about his father, because he was letting her see the things he couldn't say out loud. The way he'd looked at her mouth and not looked away until the world interrupted, because he wanted her, fully, and hadn't even tried to hide it.

She reached for his hand. Their fingers met, and the bond flared, like a second heartbeat in her chest. She didn't know if it was the bond or just him, the warmth in his palm, the way his fingers tightened around hers instinctively, but it was enough. She was close to him. Close enough to feel his breath, close enough to feel the rumble of his chest against her arm. Close enough to trust that he wasn't going anywhere.

He didn't say anything. He didn't need to. He just held her hand, and the silence between them was filled with everything they couldn't put into words.

They sat on the roof until the stars came out. Neither of them said anything. They just sat there, two people who were suddenly, terrifyingly close.

He stood first. Didn't say goodbye. Just nodded, and walked down the stairs, and she stood there alone until his footsteps faded into the night.

When she went back inside, she didn't close her eyes for the first time in weeks and think about being alone.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 19: The Border Dispute

VICTOR

The bond pulled him before he even opened his eyes.

It wasn't a sound, or a word, or an image. It was warmth, a steady, low hum, like a second heartbeat in his chest. Amelia was awake. He didn't know how he knew, but he knew, and it made something loosen in his ribs that had been knotted tight for weeks.

He got up. Washed his face. Put on his coat. Didn't need to go looking for her. The bond hummed, steady and open, and that was enough.

He walked into the compound's common room at eight AM and David was already there, coffee in hand, eyes narrow.

"You look different," David said.

Victor sat at the table. "How?"

"I don't know. You look — I don't know. Less tired." David studied him. "Is it the bond?"

"David."

"I'm just asking. You've been —" He gestured vaguely. "Running yourself into the ground since your father died. But you look different. Better. Or worse. I can't tell."

Victor didn't answer. He couldn't tell either. He felt steady. That was the word. Steady. For the first time in three years, he felt steady. And it terrified him.

The war room filled quickly. Perseus arrived with maps. Emila came with supply reports. Josiah stood by the window, arms crossed, quiet as always.

Then Marco walked in with news.

"Alpha," he said, and his voice was tight. "Border runners from the south. They've been pushed back three miles in the last week. Hanley's men are moving fast."

The room shifted. Shoulders straightened. Voices dropped. Everyone knew what that meant.

Gage Hanley. Alpha of the rival wolf pack, three hundred miles south in Louisiana swampland. Broad, scarred, aggressive. Believed wolves were superior to all other shifter types. Believed the Blackwood Pack's willingness to work with other shifter types, to bond with them, was degeneracy.

And now he was testing the northern boundary. Pushing. Looking for weakness.

Perseus spread the map on the table and pointed at the southern border. "They're not attacking. Not yet. They're probing. Testing our response time. How fast we mobilize. Whether we fight back or hold the line."

"David's men are there?" Victor said.

"Yes," David said. "But they're light on numbers. If Hanley pushes harder, we'll need a senior warrior to take command."

"I'll go," Victor said.

No one answered immediately. Perseus looked at him.

"You're the Alpha," he said quietly. "You hold the center. I'll handle the edges."

"You don't have to handle —"

"Victor." Perseus's voice was firm but not unkind. "You hold the center. I handle the edges. That's what you taught me. That's what this pack needs from you right now."

Victor sat back in his chair and nodded. It was the right call. It was the responsible call. But it didn't stop something cold from opening in his chest, because the edges felt important now. The bond had made him hyper-aware of everything outside his immediate circle. He felt the pack's tension like pressure in his skull. He felt David's need to act, Emila's worry, Josiah's quiet calculation. He felt Perseus's certainty.

And he felt Amelia. Somewhere down the road, she was waking up, and the bond hummed like a second heartbeat in his chest, and he wondered if she could feel his attention, the way his gaze kept returning to the southern border like it was a threat he couldn't name.

Perseus rolled up the map and stood. "I leave by noon."

David nodded. Emila packed supplies. Josiah disappeared without a word.

But Perseus didn't head for the door. He walked over to Victor and leaned in close, his voice low enough that no one else could hear.

"You're distracted," he said.

Victor looked up at him.

"The bond," Perseus said quietly. "Is it affecting your judgment?"

Victor didn't answer. He couldn't. Not because he didn't have an answer, but because he didn't have one at all. He felt distracted. He felt, he felt everything. The bond had opened something in him, made him aware of things he'd spent years burying under duty and control and the weight of being Alpha. And now he couldn't close them back up.

Perseus watched him for a long moment. Then he nodded once and turned away.

The nod wasn't angry. It wasn't accusatory. But it was doubt. And it was the kind of doubt that grew in the quiet, in the moments between words, in the spaces where a man was supposed to be certain and instead was just uncertain.

When the war room emptied, Victor stood alone at the table, staring at the map and trying to figure out what Perseus meant.

---

AMELIA

Amelia's guest room was quiet in the morning. This was not the heavy, oppressive quiet that had settled over her for weeks, the quiet of an empty house, an empty bed, an empty life. This was different. This was the quiet of a woman who had slept through the night for the first time in months and woken up without that familiar ache in her chest.

She made coffee. She sat at her kitchen table with her research notes. She looked at the shelf where she'd placed her grandmother's packet, the one that smelled like fresh pancakes and bacon and her grandmother's kitchen. She had put it there the night before, after the kitchen scene, after he'd offered her a room and looked at her mouth and not looked away.

Luna sat on her lap, purring, tail flicking in slow, contented circles.

"You like it here," Amelia said. Luna didn't answer. She was a cat. Cats didn't answer questions that required yes or no. But she purred louder, which was close enough.

Amelia picked up one of her research notes and tried to read it. She had been studying historical records of mate bonds: patterns, timing, correlations with Hollow King activity. She'd found something troubling: every time a mate bond was formed, the Hollow King struck harder within weeks. It was a pattern, but it wasn't a correlation she understood. Was the Hollow King hunting bonds? Or was he just drawn to them, like moths to flame?

She didn't have an answer. But she suspected the answer was bad.

She set the notes down and stared at the wall.

The bond hummed in her chest, low and steady. She could feel him, not his thoughts, not his emotions, just his presence. A warmth, a steadiness, a hum that felt like a second heartbeat. It was comforting. It was also confusing. Because it made her feel less alone, and less alone was exactly what she had been trying to manage. She didn't want to get used to this. She didn't want to get used to having someone who hummed in her chest like a second heartbeat.

But she did. That was the problem. She did want it.

---

VICTOR

Perseus left at noon. Victor walked him to the gate and watched him ride south, his cloak flapping in the wind, his horse moving fast across the flat ground. The bond hummed, Amelia's presence, steady and distant. He couldn't tell what she was doing. He could only feel the shape of her attention, like a pressure in his chest, warm and quiet.

He turned away and walked back to the war room.

David was still there, pacing, jaw tight, hands clenched.

"He's going to walk right into it," David said.

"Perseus?" Victor said.

"He's walking right into a negotiation with Gage Hanley. A man who thinks wolves are better than everyone else and has been looking for an excuse to test us since the day you took over. Perseus is one man. Hanley has a pack behind him."

"He's Perseus," Victor said.

"I know who he is. I'm not saying he can't handle it. I'm saying —" David stopped, ran a hand through his hair. "I'm saying you should have gone. I know you said you'd handle the center, but this is an edge. This is territory. This is—"

"David."

"You're different, Victor. I don't know if it's the bond or something else, but you're — it's like you're listening to something we're not hearing."

Victor looked at him. "What do you hear?"

"Nothing," David said. "That's the point. I hear nothing. And I'm supposed to stand here and wait while Perseus rides south to talk to a man who doesn't want to talk."

Victor didn't answer. He couldn't. Because David was right. The bond had changed the way he experienced the world. He felt things more deeply. He was aware of things he'd been blind to before. And he wasn't sure if that was strength or weakness.

He looked at the map on the table and thought of the southern border. The Hanley pack. Gage Hanley, the man who thought the Blackwood Pack was weak.

*Perseus was the right choice for the negotiation.* Victor knew it. But knowing it didn't stop the cold, tight feeling in his chest, and he wondered if it ever would.

He walked to the window and looked south.

The horizon was flat and empty. He couldn't see Perseus. He couldn't see the southern border. He could only feel the hum in his chest: Amelia's presence, steady and warm, and the faint, distant pulse of something else. A pull. A tension. Something that felt like a pack moving fast and hard, like a hundred wolves running toward a fight they didn't need to have.

The bond hummed. He closed his eyes. He tried to steady himself.

But the hum didn't stop. It didn't even fade. It stayed steady and warm, like a second heartbeat in his chest and he wondered if this was what it felt like to be alive.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 20: The Research

AMELIA

The research notes spread across her kitchen table like a puzzle she couldn't stop trying to solve.

She had been reading for three hours. Her coffee had gone cold. Luna was asleep on the counter, tail twitching in her sleep. The morning light had shifted from gold to white, and she still hadn't blinked.

The pattern was there. She could feel it in the lines she'd highlighted, in the marginalia she'd written in her own cramped handwriting, in the way her pulse was picking up even though her brain kept telling her to stay calm, to be methodical, to not jump to conclusions.

She picked up one of the historical records, a scanned page from an archive she'd reached through a mentor in Edinburgh. The text was in Latin, but she'd translated it weeks ago. It described a Mate Bond forming in the Scottish Highlands, 1642. And three months later, the same village was struck, bodies found hollowed, eyes empty, bodies breathing.

She picked up another record. Italian Alps, 1789. Mate Bond formed in a small mountain village. Five months later, the same village burned. All inhabitants gone. No bodies. No trace.

Another. The Black Sea coast, 1812. Mate Bond. One year later, a Hollow King strike that wiped out an entire pack, seventeen shifters, all hollowed, all found lined up in a row like dolls in a display case.

She picked up the last record, not from the archives, but from her own life. The Blackwood Pack. Victor. Herself. Formed. Three months ago. And in the last month, Marcus hollowed. The second hunter. The pack scared to death.

The pattern was clear. Too clear.

Every time a Mate Bond was formed, the Hollow King struck harder. Not the same day. Not the next week. But within months, always within months, he came for them.

It wasn't coincidence. It couldn't be.

She sat back in her chair and stared at the wall. Her hands were shaking.

The Hollow King didn't just fear bonds. He hunted them. He used them as a target. Every bond was a flare in the dark, and the Hollow King was drawn to it like a predator drawn to prey.

Amelia closed her eyes and pressed her palms against her eyes. She had brought this on him. She had recognized the bond, and the bond had recognized her, and now the Hollow King was coming for them because of it.

She thought about Victor standing at the window looking south, feeling the tension of the pack movement. She thought about how he'd carried the weight of the pack for three years since his father died, alone, never burdening anyone with what he was carrying. And now he was carrying this too, the bond, the Hollow King, the responsibility, the fear.

She didn't want him to carry it alone. Not anymore.

She packed her notes into a satchel. She put her coffee mug in the sink. She told Luna she'd be back soon. And she walked out the door.

---

VICTOR

The war room was quiet when Amelia walked in.

She didn't knock. She didn't call out. She just pushed the door open and walked in like she had every right to be there, because she did. She had earned the right to be in this room months ago, when she'd looked at him across the clearing and told him she didn't know what was happening to her, but she wasn't going anywhere.

David was there. Josiah was there. Emila was there. And when Amelia walked in, all of them went quiet.

He noticed that. He noticed the way their eyes shifted toward her, the way the room seemed to rearrange itself around her presence. It was the same reaction he'd seen a hundred times, his own mood shifting the moment she walked in, the way his guard came down. And the pack noticed it too.

"You're late," he said.

She smiled, small, amused, just enough to make his chest tighten. "I was researching."

"Research can wait."

"No," she said. "It can't."

And then she put her satchel on the table, pulled out a stack of papers, and started talking.

She laid out the historical records one by one. The Scottish Highlands, 1642. The Italian Alps, 1789. The Black Sea coast, 1812. The Blackwood Pack, three months ago. Each one had a Mate Bond followed by a Hollow King strike. Within months. Always within months.

The pattern was clear.

The war room was silent. David's jaw was tight. Josiah's expression was unreadable. Emila's eyes were soft.

And Victor, Victor felt the cold open in his chest, the same cold that had opened when Perseus had questioned him about the bond affecting his judgment. But this was worse. This wasn't doubt. This was confirmation.

The Hollow King wasn't just coming for the pack. He was coming for the bond. He was hunting bonds.

And the bond had found Amelia.

"You're telling me," Victor said, and his voice was low, controlled, but there was something in it — something sharp, something dangerous — "that every time a Mate Bond was formed, the Hollow King struck? That's not coincidence?"

"No," Amelia said. "It's not coincidence."

"And you think he targets bonds specifically?"

"Yes."

"Because they're a threat to him?"

"Yes."

Victor looked at the papers. He looked at Amelia. He looked at the war room, at David, at Josiah, at Emila, and he felt the weight of it all settle on his shoulders like a stone.

He was being used. By the Hollow King, who saw the bond as a weakness to exploit. By Aurela, who saw the bond as a weapon to deploy. There was no neutral ground. There never had been.

He was furious. Not at Amelia. Never at her. He was furious at the situation, at the Hollow King, at the Moon Goddess, at the bond, at the fact that there was no way to protect the pack without putting Amelia in the crosshairs.

"You did the right thing," he said finally. "You found this, and you brought it to me."

"I didn't know who else to—"

"You did the right thing," he said again, firmer this time. "Amelia."

She nodded. Her hands were still. Her face was steady. But he could read the tension in her eyes, the quiet fear she wasn't saying out loud.

He wanted to tell her to stop worrying. He wanted to tell her she was safe. But he couldn't. Because she wasn't safe. None of them were. And saying it would be a lie.

He said nothing. He just nodded once, turned back to the map, and tried to figure out what to do next.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 21: The Second Attack

AMELIA

She was in the compound when it happened.

Not by invitation. Not by plan. She'd come to return something, one of her herbal notes that Victor had picked up in the war room weeks ago. She didn't even remember why she'd thought to bring it back. Maybe because she'd noticed he'd been using it as a bookmark for his patrol reports. Maybe because it felt like a small, stupid thing that made her feel useful.

She was sitting in the healing tent talking to Emila about wound care when the alarm sounded.

It wasn't a horn or a shout. It was the way the air changed, a pressure drop, like the moment before a storm. Emila stopped mid-sentence. Her head tilted. Her eyes went wide.

"I feel it too," she said quietly.

She didn't know what had happened, but she didn't need to. She just got up and ran toward the healing tent.

---

VICTOR

He felt it first as a pressure behind his eyes. Then a cold wave, like ice water poured down his spine. Then the smell, not blood, not fire, but something worse. Something empty. Something that smelled like absence.

He was in his quarters, sitting at his desk, when Marco burst through the door.

"Alpha," he said, and his voice was shaking. "The healing tent. Someone — I don't — I can't—"

Victor was already moving. He was out of the door, down the hall, across the compound, with Marco right behind him. They arrived at the edge of the healing tent and Victor stopped. He wanted to look away. He couldn't. He was staring at a face he knew. A face he'd grown up with. A face he'd laughed with, fought with, bled with.

Reid's face. Not Reid's body. His body was there, breathing, eyes open, chest rising and falling. But his eyes were empty. His mind was gone.

Hollowed.

Victor recognized him. It was Reid Beaumont. Perseus's younger brother. Victor's oldest friend, they'd known each other since they were pups in the same litter. They'd trained together for years. Reid had stood beside him during his first pack council. He'd joked about retiring to the southern border and opening a tavern.

Reid was reaching for him. Movement mechanical and wrong, like a puppet with strings cut halfway.

Victor didn't want to. He couldn't. But he had to. He put him down.

He didn't cry. He didn't make a sound. He just stood there with his hands shaking and his chest aching, trying to understand how something like this had happened inside the compound. Inside the wards. Inside the one place that was supposed to be safe.

The pack was on lockdown. No one in. No one out. The wards were reinforced. The guards doubled. But the question hung in the air like smoke. How did this happen?

Someone opened the wards. Someone invited the Hollow King in.

And Victor couldn't figure out who.

---

AMELIA

She found him in the healing tent, standing over Reid's body, staring at something only he could see. He hadn't moved. He hadn't blinked. He was just standing there, his hands clenched into fists at his sides, his shoulders tense like he was holding up the entire world.

She didn't ask what happened. She didn't try to understand. She just walked over and sat down beside him.

Not touching. Not yet. Just there.

Victor didn't say anything. She didn't say anything. They sat in silence for a long time. The healers worked around them. Emila moved quietly, her face drawn, her hands steady. Josiah stood by the door, his eyes closed, his expression unreadable.

Then Victor spoke. His voice was low, broken, barely a whisper.

"He was my friend," he said. "I trained with him. Since we were pups. I had to put him down. I had to."

And Amelia, she didn't say anything. She didn't offer platitudes. She didn't tell him it wasn't his fault. She just reached for his hand. And when he let her hold it, she held on.

Not tightly. Not desperately. Just firmly. Steadily. The way you hold something you don't want to lose.

And for the first time, Victor let someone see him break.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 22: The Suspects

AMELIA

She found him on the roof again.

She didn't announce herself. She just climbed up the access ladder and walked over. He was sitting on the edge, his head in his hands, his shoulders tense, his silence louder than any words he could have spoken. She sat down beside him, close enough to feel the warmth of his body but not close enough to touch. She didn't assume. He hadn't given permission.

He didn't look up. He didn't speak. He just sat there, staring at nothing.

She didn't ask how he was. He wasn't okay, and the question was too small for what he was carrying.

Instead she said: "The pack needs you."

And he said: "The pack doesn't need me to see what I've failed to protect."

She didn't argue. She just sat with him until he stopped shaking.

When he finally stood, she stepped back. He didn't say goodbye. He just climbed down the access ladder and walked away. She watched him go until he disappeared around the corner.

---

VICTOR

Perseus rode back into the compound at dusk. He was tired, his clothes dusty from the road, his expression unreadable. The Hanley negotiation had gone as well as could be expected, with Gage Hanley stubborn, traditional, and not impressed by Blackwood diplomacy. But Perseus had kept it from breaking down. Barely.

He didn't go to his quarters. He went straight to the healing tent. And that was where he found Marco standing at the edge of the tent, staring at something he couldn't bring himself to look at. He couldn't look away.

Marco's head snapped up when Perseus approached. He opened his mouth to speak, but no words came out. He just shook his head. He stepped aside, gesturing past him toward the cot.

Perseus looked past him.

Reid's face. Not Reid's body. His body was there, breathing, eyes open, chest rising and falling. But his eyes were empty. His mind was gone.

Hollowed.

Perseus stared at his younger brother, at the face he'd grown up with, the face he'd defended, the face he'd told to go south and open a tavern when Reid complained about pack life.

He didn't make a sound. He didn't move. He just stood there, his hands clenched into fists at his sides, his throat tight, his vision blurring.

Victor was there too, standing a few feet away, his face pale, his hands still. Victor had put Reid down. Victor had done it with his own hands.

Perseus looked at his Alpha, looked at his friend, and saw the weight of it. The crushing, immovable weight of a man who had put down someone he loved.

And then he turned and walked away. No words. No shouting. Just silence.

---

AMELIA

Perseus led the investigation. He was methodical, talking to every pack member who had been in the compound that day, checking the ward logs, reviewing the patrol routes. He didn't suspect anyone. Not yet. But he wasn't ruling anyone out either.

David wanted to punish everyone. He paced the war room, his fists clenched, his voice loud enough to shake the walls. "Lock them all down. Double the guards. Search every tent. If someone opened the wards, they'll be hiding something."

Josiah wanted to wait. He sat quietly in the corner, his hands folded in his lap, his eyes closed. "Panic is the Hollow King's first weapon," he said. "If we punish people for fear, we're giving him exactly what he wants."

Victor said nothing. He sat at the head of the table, his face unreadable, his silence louder than David's shouting. But Amelia could see his hands, resting flat on the table, trembling slightly, his knuckles white.

She watched the meeting from the door. She watched Perseus work. She watched David lose control. She watched Josiah sit calmly in the eye of the storm. And she watched Victor break, quietly, internally, completely.

When the meeting broke, she followed him outside.

He didn't notice her at first. He was walking toward the compound wall, his hands shoved in his pockets, his head down. She caught up to him and stepped into his path.

He stopped. He looked at her. His eyes were red-rimmed. His face was drawn. And he looked at her like he had nowhere left to go.

"You're punishing yourself," she said. "That doesn't help them."

He closed his eyes. His jaw tightened. When he opened them again, his voice was barely a whisper.

"I should have seen it. I should have been there. I should have—"

"You can't carry everything alone, Victor."

He didn't answer. He couldn't. He just looked at her with eyes full of everything he couldn't say.

And she knew then, not with magic, not with the bond, but with her own two eyes, that the Hollow King hadn't just hollowed Reid. He had hollowed Victor too. Not yet. But close.

---

VICTOR

He couldn't look at certain pack members without seeing Reid's face. Every time David laughed at something, a joke, a memory, something stupid and human and alive, it made his chest ache. Every time Marco walked past him, whistling some old tune, it made his hands shake. Every time Emila asked how he was holding up, and he said "fine," it made something inside him twist.

He was supposed to be the Alpha. The strong one. The one who held everything together. But all he could see was Reid's face. And all he could feel was the weight of a body he had put down.

He walked the perimeter of the compound alone. One step at a time. Eyes forward. Shoulders straight. But his hands were shaking, and his chest was tight, and the silence in his head was deafening.

He didn't know who had opened the wards. He didn't know who had let the Hollow King in. And he didn't know how to move forward when every step reminded him that he had failed, failed Reid, failed the pack, failed the person he was supposed to be.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 23: The Full Moon

AMELIA

She felt it at dawn, the same pull she'd felt before, but stronger. The thread between them was taut, vibrating like a plucked string. She knew what it meant before she even thought about it: the moon was coming.

She didn't fight it. She'd learned that lesson already. Instead, she made tea, fed Luna, and sat by the window of her guest room until the sun had climbed high enough to make the walk feel manageable. Her pulse kept spiking without reason, her body humming with anticipation she couldn't name. She waited until mid-morning before she finally stood, pulled on her coat, and left the compound by the eastern gate. The forest grew darker as she got closer, the air thick with that low vibration she'd learned to recognize. Her hands tingled before she even saw the trees.

When she arrived, he was already there.

Victor stood at the center of the clearing, his back to her, his head tilted toward the sky. The moonlight caught the edge of his profile, and for a moment she just watched him, the way his shoulders were tense, the way his hands hung at his sides, the way he looked like a man carrying the weight of the world on his back.

He turned when she stepped into the clearing. His eyes were gray, darker than usual, blown wide with something raw. And the thread between them pulled so tight it ached.

She didn't say anything. He didn't either. They just stood there, twenty feet apart, the moonlight between them, the bond humming so loud she could hear it in her teeth. Her skin felt too tight. Her breath came faster. She had to force herself to stay where she was.

Then he spoke. His voice was low, rough around the edges. "I didn't think I'd see you."

She stepped closer. "You called me here."

He looked down. "I didn't call you. The bond did."

"That's the same thing."

He didn't argue. He just stood there, his hands in his pockets, his shoulders stiff, his gaze fixed on the ground. She noticed that he was shaking, just slightly, barely visible, but she noticed. The sight of it sent something hot through her chest.

"You're scared," she said.

His jaw tightened. "I'm the Alpha. I don't get to be scared."

"That doesn't mean you aren't."

He looked at her then, really looked at her, and something in his face cracked. Just a little. But it was enough.

"I'm scared of failing them," he said. "Of failing the pack. Of failing you. Of failing Reid."

The name hung between them like a stone. Reid was gone. He'd been put down. The pack was burying him at dawn tomorrow. The pack knew. Everyone knew. But saying it out loud made it real.

"I know," she said.

They stood in silence for a long time. The bond hummed between them, steady and warm. A pull she couldn't explain. She wanted to touch his hand, his arm, his shoulder. She wanted to feel him close. She took a step forward. Then another. Until she was standing right in front of him, close enough to see the pulse jumping at his throat.

He didn't move. He just stood there, his hands still in his pockets, his breathing shallow, his eyes locked on hers.

She reached up and touched his face. Her fingers found his jaw, warm and solid under her palm. He closed his eyes for half a second, like he was fighting something, like every instinct in him was screaming at him to do something, anything, and he was holding himself back by sheer force of will.

Then he moved. He pulled his hands from his pockets and held them out, palms up, open. An invitation.

She took them.

The contact was immediate, warmth flooding through her palms, up her arms, into her chest. She felt his heartbeat through his skin, fast, unsteady, controlled but barely. She felt the pulse at his wrists, the rough texture of his skin, the warmth of his hands enveloping hers. She didn't try to feel anything else. She didn't push. She just held his hands and let the warmth do what it could.

But she didn't let go. She held on, and she watched him watching her, and she saw something in his eyes that made her breath catch, something dark and desperate and hungry that she knew was reflected in her own gaze.

He looked down at their hands. His thumb moved, just once, brushing against her knuckles. Then he looked up at her.

"I don't know if I can do this," he said. His voice was lower now, rougher. "Being an Alpha. Being your mate. Being both at the same time."

"Then don't do it alone."

He stepped closer, still holding her hands. One step. Until she could feel the heat radiating off his body, until the space between them was nothing at all. His hands tightened on hers, just slightly, like he was fighting the urge to move them higher, to pull her against him. But he didn't. He just held on, the bond between them tightening like a wire drawn taut.

She tilted her face up. He didn't hesitate. He didn't think about it. He just leaned down and captured her mouth with his, and the kiss was everything she'd been waiting for and more, warm and sure and desperate, his hands finding her waist, pulling her closer, her fingers curling into his shirt. The bond flared between them, not opening, not fully, but widening, the thread stretching tight, and she could feel his heartbeat through his chest against hers, steady and fast and entirely his.

When they broke apart, it was only because he needed to breathe. His forehead rested against hers, his hands still on her waist, his breath warm against her skin. He didn't pull away. Neither did she.

"I want you," he said, and his voice was low and quiet and entirely honest. "I want you close. I want you here. But I won't cross a line you haven't let me cross."

"I know," she whispered.

He didn't move. He just stood there, his hands on her waist, his breath on her lips, his eyes locked on hers, and she could feel the restraint in him like a physical thing, a wire holding back something wild and powerful and entirely his.

They stood like that for a long time. Then he pulled back, slowly, reluctantly. He pressed one last kiss to her temple, his palm resting flat against her shoulder for a heartbeat, before he let his hand fall.

They walked back together, not holding hands, but close enough that the thread between them pulled taut with every step. She could feel the ghost of his touch on her waist, the warmth of his mouth on hers, the way his breath had felt against her lips. She didn't look back at the clearing. He didn't either. They just walked, side by side, his arm brushing against hers with every step.

---

VICTOR

He had been waiting at the clearing for an hour before she arrived. He hadn't planned to wait. He hadn't meant to. He just stood there, pacing, his hands in his pockets, his mind racing through the same loop of worst-case scenarios he'd been running since Reid died.

The bond had pulled him here, not forcefully, not like the first time, but gently, like a current carrying him toward something he knew he needed but wasn't sure he was ready for. The full moon was rising. The clearing was where they'd stood the first time. The place where Aurela had spoken. The place where everything changed.

When Amelia appeared at the edge of the clearing, he felt it, the thread tightening, the warmth spreading through his chest, the pull that he had stopped fighting a long time ago. He turned. He couldn't not turn. She was there. And the bond between them hummed like a live wire.

He didn't know how to start. He never knew how to start with her. She saw too much. She asked the right questions. And she didn't flinch when he gave her the wrong answers.

He looked at her and thought about Reid. His brother. His best friend. The one person who knew what it was like to grow up in his shadow, who knew what it was like to carry that weight and pretend it didn't crush you. Reid had died hollow, and he'd been the one to end him. His oldest friend. His littermate. He'd held Reid's body while it went cold.

That knowledge sat in his chest like a stone.

When Amelia stepped closer, he could feel the heat of her body from ten feet away. She kept coming. He wanted to stop her. He wanted her to keep going. He wanted everything and nothing and both at the same time.

He reached for her, his hands still trembling. She didn't notice. Or if she did, she didn't say anything. She just took them, and the warmth was immediate, flooding through his palms, up his arms, into his chest. He felt her pulse. He felt the steady rhythm of her heartbeat through his skin. He didn't try to feel anything else. He didn't push. He just held her hands and let it be enough.

But it wasn't enough. It was never enough.

She didn't let go. She held on, and he watched her watching him, and he saw something in her eyes that made something primal and desperate rise inside him, hunger, need, a want so sharp it was almost physical.

He looked down at their hands. He didn't want to lose this, not the contact, not the warmth, not the certainty that for this one moment, he wasn't alone. He squeezed her hands. Not tightly. Not desperately. Just firmly. Steadily.

Then she stepped closer, still holding her hands. One step. Until the space between them was nothing at all. He could feel the heat of her body, smell the scent of her skin, see the pulse jumping at her throat. His hands tightened on hers, just slightly, like he was fighting the urge to move them higher, to pull her against him. But he didn't. He just held on, the bond between them tightening like a wire drawn taut.

And then he kissed her.

He didn't plan it. He didn't think about it. He just leaned down and captured her mouth with his, and she was warm and soft and real and she tasted like tea and something uniquely her, and he pulled her closer, his hands finding her waist, her fingers curling into his shirt, the bond flaring between them, not opening, not fully, but widening, the thread stretching taut, and he could feel her heartbeat against his chest, fast and steady and entirely hers.

When they broke apart, it was only because he needed to breathe. His forehead rested against hers, his hands still on her waist, his breath warm against her skin. He didn't pull away. Neither did she.

He could feel her pulse under his fingertips where his hands rested on her waist. He could feel the heat of her skin against his chest. He could feel the way she leaned into him without thinking about it, like she belonged there, like she was meant to be there, and it nearly broke him.

"I want you," he said, and his voice was low and quiet and entirely honest. "I want you close. I want you here. But I won't cross a line you haven't let me cross."

"I know," she whispered.

He didn't move. He just stood there, his hands on her waist, his breath on her lips, his eyes locked on hers, and he could feel the restraint in him like a physical thing, a wire holding back something wild and powerful and entirely his.

They stood like that for a long time. Then he pulled back, slowly, reluctantly. He pressed one last kiss to her temple, his palm resting flat against her shoulder for a heartbeat, before he let his hand fall.

They walked back together, not holding hands, but close enough that the thread between them pulled taut with every step. He could feel the ghost of her mouth on his, the warmth of her body against his arm, the way she leaned into him with every step. He didn't look back at the clearing. She didn't either. They just walked, side by side, with the thread pulling taut between them with every step, and he felt something he hadn't felt in a long time.

Not relief. Not yet. But hope. Small. Fragile. Real.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 24: The Traitor Revealed

PERSEUS

The reports didn't add up. He'd been going over them for three days, and they still didn't add up.

On paper, the ward breach looked like sabotage. The outer ward had been disengaged from the inside. Someone knew the sequence. Someone had the knowledge. That meant betrayal. It had to. The ward protocols weren't public. They were Alpha-level. Only the inner council knew them.

But Victor was Alpha. Victor gave the orders. Victor was the only one who could authorize the sequence. Unless someone had copied it. Or someone had learned it from him.

Perseus had ruled out copying. The ward logs showed no unauthorized access. The sequence had been executed by someone standing at the control node inside the compound. That meant they were physically present. Inside the walls. At the moment of breach.

He'd interviewed every member of the inner council. He'd checked the logs. He'd walked the perimeter himself. He'd done everything a good second-in-command could do, and every answer pointed to betrayal.

He wasn't ready to accept that. Not yet.

So he went back to the evidence. The physical evidence. The thing he hadn't looked at closely enough.

The control node was a stone pedestal near the healing tent. It was where ward adjustments were made. Routine maintenance, seasonal recalibrations, emergency overrides. The Hollow King attack had happened when someone had overridden the outer ward. They'd disengaged it, let something through, and then let the ward snap back into place behind it.

Clean. Professional. If you knew what you were doing.

Perseus knelt in front of the node. He'd walked past it a dozen times since the attack. A dozen times without really looking. But this time he saw it.

He knelt. He pressed his hand against the stone and felt the residual magic. Cold, wrong, like standing next to a body that had just been put down. And then he felt something else.

There was a trace of something foreign. Not human. Not shifter. Not anything he recognized. It was faint, almost gone, like a scent after the rain had washed it away. But it was there. And it wasn't disengaging magic. Someone had to stand at the node to disengage the ward. This was different. This was the ward being disengaged. From the outside.

His breath caught.

The ward hadn't been sabotaged from the inside. The Hollow King had done it from the outside.

He'd used his Void Power. Not to override the sequence, but to weaken the ward itself, to thin it enough that something could push through. No one had to stand at the node. No one had to be present. The breach was impossible. And that was exactly the point.

Perseus stood up. His hands were shaking.

David was right. Perseus had been looking for a traitor inside the compound. He'd spent three days interrogating pack members, watching their faces, looking for guilt. He'd turned the pack against each other. He'd made them suspicious of their own brothers and sisters.

The truth was simpler and more terrifying. The Hollow King had done this alone.

He walked to the war room and found Victor at the head of the table, David across from him with his head in his hands, Josiah watching from the corner.

Perseus put his papers on the table. "I was wrong."

Victor looked up. "About what?"

"The breach. It wasn't sabotage." Perseus met Victor's eyes. "It was the Hollow King. He weakened the ward from the outside. His Void Power. He didn't need a traitor. He didn't need someone inside the walls."

The room went still.

David pushed back from the table. "That's not possible. The wards are Alpha-rated. Nothing gets through them unless someone opens them."

"Not unless the thing on the other side is strong enough to push through," Perseus said. "The Hollow King doesn't break in. He dissolves the lock."

Victor stared at the table. His jaw worked. He looked relieved. Perseus could see it. But the relief was followed by something darker. Horror.

"He can enter minds," Victor said quietly. "He can manipulate fear. He can turn what we're afraid of against us."

Perseus nodded. "That's what this is. He didn't need a traitor because the breach was always going to be impossible. That's how he gets inside. He makes the impossible real."

Josiah closed his eyes. "The old stories said he didn't just eat bodies. He ate certainty. He made people doubt that they could trust their own eyes, their own senses. Once they doubted, the wards meant nothing."

Victor stood and walked to the window. He didn't turn around.

"So it wasn't any of you," he said to the room. To Perseus. To David. To Josiah.

"No," Perseus said. "It was never any of us."

Victor was quiet for a long time. Then he said something that surprised them all.

"Thank you."

It was the first time anyone had said that to him since Reid died.

He turned around. His face was pale, but steady. "If the Hollow King can get through the wards without help, that means he can come back. Any time. Anywhere."

David pushed back from the table. "Then we fortify. We reinforce the wards and we double the patrols."

Perseus shook his head. "Reinforcing won't help. If he can dissolve them from the outside, reinforcing just gives him more material to dissolve. We need to understand what he's capable of. We need to know if his power is infinite or if there's a limit."

Josiah opened his eyes. "There's always a limit. Everything has a limit."

"Then find it," Perseus said. "Before he does."

Victor nodded. He turned back to the window. He looked over the compound, over the pack, and the lives that depended on him. The weight of it was crushing, but it was real now. Not a monster in the woods. Not a shadow on the horizon. A predator of the mind.

And for the first time, he understood what he was fighting.

AMELIA

Amelia stood in the kitchen, waiting for tea to steep, listening to the sound of voices coming from the war room. She'd been trying to ignore it. The tension. The low arguing. The way the bond hummed with Victor's stress. But she couldn't. Not anymore.

She'd moved into the guest room weeks ago. Since then, every morning she woke up feeling him through the bond. Just the hum. The low steady vibration of his presence. Like a second heartbeat. She knew when he was awake before she opened her eyes. She knew when he was near by the way the hum got louder.

It was intimate in a way she didn't have a word for. And she didn't need one.

This morning, the hum was sharp. Tense. It made her skin feel too tight. Her pulse spiked when she thought about him carrying whatever was happening inside those walls.

She poured the tea, set the mug on the table, and stood there, listening.

The voices stopped.

Then footsteps. Heavy, close. The door opened, and Victor stepped into the kitchen.

He looked wrecked. His hair was messy. His shirt was wrinkled. His face was pale. But his eyes were gray and present and fixed on her like she was the only real thing in the room.

"You're up early," she said.

"Can't sleep," he said. His voice was rough.

She nodded. She knew what that was like. "I was making tea."

He stared at her for a long moment. She could see the weight of it in his shoulders, the way his jaw worked when he didn't want to say what he was thinking.

He moved past her to the counter, his shoulders tense, jaw working. He didn't look at her. He looked at the wall behind her.

"Perseus just told me something. The breach. It wasn't anyone from the pack. No traitor. No betrayal. It was the Hollow King. He weakened the ward from the outside. His Void Power."

Amelia's breath caught. "That's good, right?"

"Yes." He looked at her. His eyes were darker now. "It's good. It's also terrifying. Because it means he can come back. Any time. Anywhere. He doesn't need anyone to help him."

He set both hands on the counter. His shoulders were tight, jaw clenched. He didn't look at her, couldn't.

The space between them was nothing. She hadn't moved. He had.

"You're close," she said quietly.

He looked down. Looked back up. His eyes were dark. "I didn't realize."

"Don't apologize." She took a half step closer. Close enough to see the pulse jumping at his throat. "I like it."

His breath caught. Just slightly. But she noticed.

A long moment passed. The bond hummed between them, thick and warm. She let herself feel it, the heat, the pulse, the way he was holding himself back. Then she stepped back. Just a little. Breathed. Tried to get back to business.

"We can't just reinforce them," she said. "We change them."

Victor frowned. "Change them how?"

"Your wards work because they respond to the pack. To the Alphas. To the inner council. They're designed to keep things out. But if the Hollow King can dissolve them from the outside, maybe the answer isn't stronger wards. Maybe the answer is wards that respond differently."

She walked past him to the counter. He followed. Of course he followed. His body moved toward hers like the bond was a current and he was water. She picked up a scrap of paper, found a pen, and started drawing. Her hands knew the way.

He was watching her. She could feel it. The weight of his attention pressed on her skin like warmth on her face. She could feel him looking at her hands, her shoulders, the way she held the pen. He wasn't trying to hide it.
