"The bond is the strongest thing I've ever felt," she said. She set the pen down. She turned to face him. Her chest was close to his arm. She could feel the stiffness in his muscles, the way he was holding himself still. "It connects us. But it also creates a space. Our space. What if the wards don't just depend on the pack? What if they depend on the bond?"

Victor was quiet for a long time. His hand was still braced on the counter. Her body was between his hand and his body. She could feel the heat of him through her shirt.

His hand moved. Not toward her face. Not toward her waist. Toward her hand. His fingers found hers. Wrapped around them. Warm and solid and exactly what she'd been waiting for.

"Then research," he said. His voice was low. His thumb moved, just once, brushing against her knuckles. "And let me know what you find."

She didn't let go of his hand. She held on. Felt his heartbeat through his skin. Fast. Controlled but barely.

He looked down at their joined hands. Then up at her. His eyes were dark. Hungry. Holding back.

"I need to go," he said. His voice was rougher.

She nodded. She didn't let go right away. She held on for one more heartbeat. Two. Then she let her hand fall.

He turned to leave. He was at the door. He stopped. He didn't turn around.

"I'm glad you're here," he said.

And then he was gone. She stood there, listening to his footsteps fade. Her hand still tingled where he'd held it. The bond hummed between them. Steady. Warm. Pulling.

She picked up her tea. It was cold, but she drank it anyway.

---

Later that afternoon, Amelia met Josiah in the garden behind the healing tent. He was sitting on a bench, hands folded, eyes closed. He looked peaceful. He looked old.

She sat beside him. He didn't open his eyes.

"You have a question," he said.

"I have ten thousand," she said.

"Good. That means you're paying attention."

She told him about the wards, the bond barrier, Aurela's words, and Perseus's discovery.

Josiah listened without interrupting. When she finished, he opened his eyes.

"You're thinking about this the right way," he said. "The Hollow King is a predator of the mind. He feeds on fear. He creates doubt. And doubt makes the wards weak. That's why he can dissolve them. It's not just power. It's psychological. He makes the wards doubt themselves."

"That doesn't make sense," Amelia said. "Wards don't have minds."

"Then think of them as mirrors," Josiah said. "The wards reflect the pack's certainty. When the pack is united, the wards are strong. When the pack is afraid, the wards are weak. The Hollow King doesn't break the wards. He breaks the pack. And the wards follow."

Amelia absorbed this. It made sense. It made terrible sense. "So the bond could strengthen them?"

"The bond is the strongest form of certainty," Josiah said. "Two people who trust each other completely. That's something the Hollow King can't touch. He can whisper to them, he can prey on their fear, but he can't break what they've chosen. Not if they've chosen it freely."

She looked at the garden. The flowers were blooming. Small white things. She didn't know their names.

"We need to build wards that respond to the bond," she said. "Not just the pack. The bond."

Josiah nodded. "That would require understanding the bond at a deeper level. Not just the physical sensations. The emotional structure. What the bond is made of."

"I can learn that," she said.

Josiah smiled. It was the first time she'd seen him smile. It made him look younger.

"You already know more about it than most Alphas," he said. "That's because you're not afraid of it. You're not trying to control it. You're just letting it be."

He stood up. He patted her shoulder. "Keep thinking like this. And you'll be more useful to the pack than you realize."

She watched him walk away. She sat in the garden for a long time after he left. The flowers were still blooming. The hum of the bond was still in her chest. Steady. Growing.

She didn't know how to build bond-wards. But she knew one thing: she was going to try.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 25: The Decision

VICTOR

He called the pack at dawn. Not a council. Not a meeting. A gathering. Every member of the Blackwood Pack was expected to be there. Inner council, warriors, healers, scouts, cooks, guards. Everyone.

David arrived first, arms crossed, jaw tight. He'd been waiting for Victor to make a move. David always wanted Victor to make a move.

Perseus arrived second, his face unreadable, his findings in hand. Victor tore through them. The math was clean. The Hollow King had done it alone. The wards hadn't been broken from the inside. They'd been dissolved from the outside.

Josiah arrived third, his staff in hand, his eyes calm. He'd seen this before. Not the Hollow King. Not exactly. But the shape of what was coming. The weight of it. The necessity of the choice Victor was about to make.

Emila arrived fourth. She smiled at Victor when she saw him, a small thing, but it meant something. She always smiled at him. It reminded him that not everyone in this pack was afraid. Some people trusted him. That was a weight too.

The rest came in waves. Dozens of them. Then hundreds. They filled the central yard, standing shoulder to shoulder, looking up at Victor on the steps of the main building. The morning light was pale, thin, the way it was in late autumn. The air was cold. Breath hung in it.

Victor stood on the steps and looked out at them. At his pack. At the lives that depended on him. At the people he'd sworn to protect. At the people he'd failed.

Marcus. And Reid.

He pushed the names down. They sat in his chest like stones, but he pushed them down. He couldn't carry them right now. Not here.

"I called you here," he said. His voice carried. It always did. "Because I owe you the truth."

The yard went still. Even the wind seemed to stop.

"Something got through the wards," he continued. "Two of our own. Marcus at the boundary. Reid inside the compound. Someone I trained with. Someone I called my friend. I put them both down."

A murmur went through the yard. He could hear it. Quiet, pained, the kind of sound a pack makes when something sacred is taken from them. He let it happen. Let them feel it. Then he kept going.

"The investigation thought it was betrayal. Someone inside the walls. Someone who opened the door. That was wrong."

He looked at Perseus. Perseus nodded once.

"The Hollow King opened the door," Victor said. "He dissolved the wards from the outside. His Void Power. He doesn't need help. He doesn't need a traitor. He can come back. Any time."

A larger murmur. This one louder. This one edged with fear. Fear was the Hollow King's weapon. Victor knew that now. He could feel it radiating off the crowd like heat off stone.

"He can enter minds," Victor said. "He can manipulate fear. He can turn what you're afraid of against you. He's not a monster in the woods. He's a predator of the mind."

David stepped forward. His face was red, his hands clenched. "So what do we do? Do we fortify? Do we reinforce the wards? Do we find him and kill him?"

"We can't reinforce them," Perseus said from the steps beside Victor. "Not if he can dissolve them from the outside. Reinforcing just gives him more to dissolve."

"Then what?" David's voice was sharp. "What's the plan?"

Josiah stepped forward. He leaned on his staff, his eyes on the crowd, his voice soft but carrying. "There is no plan. Not yet. There is only a strategy. And a strategy requires a choice."

Victor looked at him. Josiah's eyes met his. There was nothing in them that asked him to rush. Nothing that asked him to be afraid. Just patience. Just trust.

"I know Aurela," Victor said. The name rippled through the crowd. Some knew it. Some didn't. All of them felt it. The Moon Goddess. The one who created their kind. The one who had been silent for centuries. "She speaks to me. In dreams. In visions. She told me why the Mate Bond exists. It is not for romance. It is not for connection. It is a weapon. A barrier. Bonds between different shifter types create walls the Hollow King cannot cross. My bond to Amelia. A human. A shifter. Is the first wall. It is not enough. But it is real."

He paused. He looked out at them. At his people. At their fear, their doubt, their need for certainty.

"I am not asking you to believe in a goddess," he said. "I am asking you to believe in me."

Silence.

Then Josiah stepped forward and knelt. Just one knee. Just one. But it was enough. He placed his staff on the ground. He looked up at Victor with eyes that had seen centuries.

"I believe," he said.

David hesitated. He looked at Josiah. He looked at Victor. He looked at Perseus, who gave a slow nod. Then David stepped forward and knelt. One knee.

"I believe," he said.

One by one, they followed. Warriors. Scouts. Healers. Guards. Dozens of them. Some knelt. Some bowed their heads. Some just stood there, eyes on Victor, waiting for him to say what came next.

And then Victor said it.

"I will complete the bond."

The word landed like a stone in still water. Ripples. Then silence.

"I will perform the Moonlit Rite. The ceremony. Under the next full moon."

He didn't look away. He held their eyes.

"Not because Aurela told me to. Not because it is the right strategic move. I will do it because Amelia is my mate. And I refuse to let fear dictate my life. The ceremony is not just magic. It is a vow. And I am ready to make it."

The yard erupted. Voices, shouts, the murmur of a pack processing something enormous. Victor didn't move. He stood on the steps, his hands at his sides, his face steady, his heart hammering against his ribs.

Perseus was the first to reach him. He put a hand on Victor's shoulder. His voice was low. "Are you sure?"

Victor didn't hesitate. "Yes."

Perseus nodded. "Then we prepare. The ceremony requires preparation. Time. Space. Josiah will need room."

Victor looked at Josiah. Josiah was watching him. Waiting.

"How long?" Victor asked.

Josiah thought for a moment. "Three days. The next full moon is three days from tonight. We have time."

Victor nodded. "Then three days."

He looked out at the yard one more time. At the faces. At the fear that was slowly turning into something else. Something steadier. Something that looked like hope.

It was his. Not Aurela's. Not the Moon Goddess's. His. He had chosen it.

---

AMELIA

The pack meeting echoes through the bond like a struck bell, heavy, sudden, impossible. She's in the healing tent, sorting herbs, and the world tilts. Something just shifted. Something enormous. She grips the edge of the table and breathes. Doesn't know what it is yet. Will. Just knows.

The feeling settles. Not gone. Just quiet. She picks up the herbs she was sorting and puts them back. Her hands are steady. Good. She needs to be steady for whatever comes next.

She goes about her morning. Feeds Luna. Makes tea. The bond stays warm between them, low and steady, like a hum beneath the noise of the world. She doesn't know what to do with this. She doesn't know how to prepare. But she will. When he tells her.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 26: The Moonlit Rite

VICTOR

The sun hasn't fully set when he finds her.

He doesn't knock. He doesn't announce himself. He just stands in the doorway of the healing tent and watches her kneel at the low table, her back to him, her dark hair pulled up with a twig she found in the garden. Her fingers move over dried leaves, crushing, measuring, folding into small linen packets. She smells like sage and warm earth. The bond hums between them, steady and low, like a second heartbeat.

She looks up. Of course she knows he's there. She always knows.

"You didn't have to find me," she says.

He shakes his head. "I needed to."

He steps inside. The tent is small, warm, crowded with the smell of dried herbs and crushed roots and something sweet he can't name. The bond hums louder when he gets closer. It always does. But tonight it feels different, different from last time, different from the last time before that. It feels like it's waiting.

She stands. She doesn't move away. The space between them closes without either of them stepping. Just enough to feel the heat of each other, the warmth of the bond, the way his breath catches when she doesn't pull back.

He looks at her. Really looks at her. Her hands are dusted with dried petals. Her wrists are bare, no gloves, no sleeves. The crescent moon tattoo catches the lantern light. Her eyes are brown, warm, steady. She's not afraid. That's the thing about her, she's never afraid of him.

"I told them," he says. "I told the pack. I told them I would complete the bond. That I would perform the ceremony. Under the full moon."

She nods. Her face is careful. Not cold. Not distant. Just careful, like she's waiting for him to say something that might change everything.

"And?" she asks.

He holds her gaze. The bond is so loud tonight. So present. Like it's vibrating through the floorboards, through the herbs on the table, through the air between them.

"And I meant it. Not because of Aurela. Not because of the Hollow King. Or any strategy." His voice is low. Steady. "Because you are my mate. And I have spent three years telling myself I am not worthy of the life my father built. I have stood in that empty room on the second floor and I have told myself I do not deserve it. I do not deserve you."

Her eyes widen. Just slightly.

"But I do. I deserve you. I deserve the bond. I deserve the ceremony. And I want to do it. Tonight."

He reaches out. His hand hovers between them. He doesn't grab. He doesn't demand. He just waits.

An open question.

She looks at his hand. Looks at his face. Looks at the space between them.

"I don't love you yet," she says.

His chest tightens. Just a little. But he doesn't look away.

"Not fully," she continues. "But I want to. And I trust you. And that is enough."

He exhales. Slow. The relief that follows is physical, like a weight he didn't know he was carrying has finally shifted.

He takes her hand. His fingers close around hers. Warm. Solid. The bond flares, not a detonation this time, not a scream. A hum. A vibration. A thread pulling taut and holding.

"Tonight," he says. "The clearing. Just us and Josiah."

She squeezes his hand. "Then let's go."

---

They walk through the tree line as the moon rises.

She doesn't wear shoes. The ground is cool, damp, the path soft with fallen leaves. She doesn't mind. The bond pulls her forward, not just toward him, toward the clearing. Toward the place where Aurela spoke. Toward the place where everything changes.

He walks beside her. Close enough that their shoulders brush. Close enough that he can feel the heat of her skin through her sleeve. Close enough that the bond hums loud enough to hear, or maybe that's just his imagination.

The clearing opens ahead of them.

The moon is full. White. Heavy. Hanging like a lantern above the trees. It floods the grass with silver light, makes the ancient stones at the center look like they're glowing. Makes the whole world feel sacred.

Josiah is already there.

He sits on a flat rock in the center of the clearing, his staff planted beside him, his eyes closed. He looks up when they approach. When he sees them together, hands linked, shoulders close, the bond glowing bright between them, he smiles. A small thing. Old. Knowing.

"You came," he says.

"We came," Amelia says.

Josiah nods. He stands. His knees crack. He's ancient. He's been doing this longer than anyone alive. Longer than Victor's father. Longer than the pack itself.

"Stand where you can see each other," he says. "The moon has to see you."

They step into the center. They face each other. Their hands are already joined. The bond surges, a wave of warmth that rolls through her chest, up her arms, into her throat. She gasps. He feels it. He gasps too.

Josiah raises his staff. The air shifts. The forest goes quiet, no crickets, no wind, no animals. Just them and the moon and the old man standing between them.

Josiah closes his eyes. His voice is low. Barely a whisper. But it carries the weight of centuries.

*"Before the Moon rose, before the Veil fell, before the shifter types forgot how to see each other, there was a time when connection was not a choice. There was a time when two souls could reach across the dark and find each other. Aurela made that possible. She built the thread. The Mate Bond. The bridge between isolation and union. The weapon against the Hollow King. The coalition of the heart."*

He pauses. His voice drops deeper. Into the old words. The real words.

# Chapter 28: The Hanley Threat Grows

VICTOR

Morning reports. That's what they are. Routine. Bureaucracy disguised as survival. Victor sits at the head of the war room table, fingers steepled, eyes on Perseus's maps. David paces. Emila refills tea he doesn't drink. Josiah watches from the corner with eyes closed. Amelia is in the healing tent. He can feel her through the bond: her warmth, the soft weight of her body, the memory of his cock inside her still echoing in her flesh. It keeps him grounded. Keeps him focused. He focuses on the reports. Northern border: clear. Supply runs: on schedule. New arrivals: three minor wolf families seeking shelter. He signs the papers. Nods. Says the right words. All the while, the bond hums in his chest, connecting him to her, to the pack, to the weight of a thousand decisions pressing down on his shoulders.

The meeting moves on. Supply inventories. Patrol rotations. Medical updates from Emila about the healing tent. Herb stocks are low, but she's managing with what she has. He nods. Signs. Repeats. But his attention is divided. Half of him is here, in the war room. The other half is with her, in the healing tent, mixing herbs, feeling the bond like a thread pulling at his chest.

Then the door opens.

Perseus steps in. His expression has changed. The casual curiosity from yesterday is gone, replaced by a sharp, calculating intensity. He carries a rolled map in one hand and a leather pouch in the other. He sets them down on the table without ceremony and unrolls the map.

"We need to talk about Hanley," he says.

David stops pacing. Perseus's voice has that effect. The room goes quiet. Even Josiah opens his eyes.

Victor leans forward. "What about him?"

Perseus unrolls the map across the table. His fingers trace a route along the southern border, past the tree line, into Shadowfen territory. "Three days ago, I went to the border to check the Hanley scout reports. What I found was worse than scouts." He taps the map. "There's a fortification. Half-built, but reinforced — palisade walls, watchtowers, supply caches. They're not pushing. They're planting flags."

David's jaw tightens. "How big?"

"Two hundred warriors. Maybe more. Gage has been building this in secret. Moving supplies from the main camp under cover of night, using Hanley sympathizers in nearby packs to transport materials. He's been at this for months." Perseus pauses. "He's also recruited the Ironridge bears. Bram Stonefist is there. Two dozen bears."

The room goes very quiet.

Victor feels the weight of the news shift the board. Wolves and bears. That's not harassment. That's preparation.

"How long?" Victor asks. "How long has it been there?"

Perseus taps a second point on the map. "About three weeks. The work started about a month ago. I missed it because Gage has eyes everywhere. But..." He pulls the leather pouch from his pocket and sets it on the table. "I found something else."

He opens the pouch. Inside: a handful of dried leaves, dark and brittle. He spreads them on the table.

"What are they?" Victor asks.

"Hollow King residue," Perseus says. "The same compound used in the hollowing process. Small amounts, but consistent. Gage isn't just building an army — he's hollowing his own warriors. Using the Hollow King's venom to make them fearless, relentless, unable to feel pain."

David's face goes white. Then red. "He's arming himself with *that*? In our territory?"

"Not our territory," Perseus corrects. "Shadowfen. But it's overlapping. And if he pushes further, it will be ours. Gage is calling the Blackwood weak. Saying we hesitated, that we won't fight back. He says our Alpha bonded with a human like a coward hiding behind magic instead of facing the Hollow King directly." He meets Victor's gaze. "He's using the bond against us. Calling it proof that you've lost your edge, Victor."

Victor feels the bond tighten in his chest. Amelia's warmth flickers: concern, sharp and sudden. He sends calm back through the bond without thinking. *I'm fine.* The warmth settles, heavy and wet with the memory of him inside her. He keeps his face neutral.

"Does anyone else know about the hollowing?" Victor asks.

Perseus shakes his head. "Only Gage's inner circle. Darius Voss is running it. I got close enough to smell it, and close enough to see the warriors. They're... not right. Their eyes are wrong. The way they move. No hesitation. No fear. Like puppets on strings."

David slams his fist on the table. "We can't just sit here and let him build an army on our border. We have to hit him now. Before he's ready. A demonstration force. Send a squadron to the fortification, show him that the Blackwood Pack is not going to back down." He turns to Victor. "Alpha."

Victor holds his friend's gaze. He can feel the weight of the room. David's anger, Perseus's calculation, Josiah's quiet attention, Emila's silence. The bond hums in his chest, connecting him to Amelia, to the pack, to the weight of a decision that will shape everything.

He considers it. The logic is sound. A demonstration force would show Hanley that the Blackwood is not weak. It would deter further expansion. But it would also be the first strike. The spark that ignites a war. And the Hollow King is still breathing down his neck. If he fights Hanley, he divides his forces. He opens himself to attack from the Hollow King's shadows.

But if he does nothing, Hanley sees it as weakness. He grows bolder. And eventually, he comes for the Blackwood regardless.

"There's more," Perseus says quietly. "Hanley is not the only threat. Darius Voss has been communicating with the Hollow King. In secret. I didn't see the communication, but I saw the traces. The same residue from the fortification is on the supply routes. Gage thinks he's playing a game. Wolves versus wolves, territory versus territory. But the Hollow King is playing him. He's using Gage to weaken both packs so he can pick up the pieces."

Victor closes his eyes. The pieces are falling into place, and none of them fit.

Hanley is building an army with Hollow King venom. The Hollow King is using Hanley as a pawn. And in the middle of it all, his pack is watching him, waiting for the Alpha to make the right call.

He opens his eyes. "David's right. We can't back down." He looks at Perseus. "But a demonstration force is too aggressive — too close to the first strike. Send a smaller group. Not warriors. Scouts. Ten men. Go to the border, show them we see what they're doing. Tell Gage that the Blackwood Pack is aware of his fortifications, and that we would prefer not to discuss them with blood." He pauses. "If he attacks them, we respond. If he doesn't, we have time to prepare."

David shakes his head. "You're sending *scouts*? Ten? Against two hundred warriors and bears?"

"Ten is enough to show we're not afraid," Victor says. "And it's not enough to be a threat. It's a message, not a battle."

Perseus considers this. Then he nods. "I'll pick the scouts. And I'll lead them." He looks at David. "We're not looking for a fight. We're looking for a statement."

David clenches his jaw. He doesn't like it. But he nods. Reluctantly. "Understood."

Josiah speaks from the corner, his voice quiet but carrying. "Gage will not listen to reason. He sees the bond as weakness because he does not understand it. He thinks love makes you soft. He does not understand that love makes you fearless." He meets Victor's gaze. "You are fearless because you have something worth protecting. That is what he fears."

Victor holds his gaze. "What should I do?"

Josiah's eyes close again. "You do what you have always done. Lead. Protect. Trust your pack. The bond does not make you weak, Victor. It makes you more than one."

DAVID

David watches from the window of the war room. Ten warriors. His jaw aches from clenching it. He chose them himself. Perseus made him choose, and David picked the best. Marcus Chen, who never flinches. Tessa Okafor, who has eyes like a hawk. Jalen Brooks, who can shift blind if he has to. They are the best the pack has. And Victor is sending them to stare down two hundred hollowed warriors and twenty bears.

*It is not enough.* The thought repeats in his head like a drumbeat. *It is never enough.*

He remembers the first war. The one before Victor became Alpha. His father, Torren Beaumont, led a full battalion against a rival pack that had grown too bold. They won. They buried three hundred dead in mass graves. He remembers the weight of the silence afterward. The way the pack had felt hollow, even in victory.

Torren died two years later. Hollow King venom in his chest. Not in battle. In betrayal. His own lieutenant, selling them out for a promise of power. David has never forgiven himself for not seeing it coming.

Now Victor is doing the same thing. Sending a handful of scouts to challenge an enemy that could crush them without thinking. *And he calls it strength.*

David's hands curl into fists. He turns away from the window. Walks back to the table. Picks up his cup. Sets it down. Picks it up again. Emila watches him with knowing eyes.

"You are going to wear a hole in the floor," she says quietly.

David forces a smile. "I know."

"It is what you do."

He looks at her. "It is what I *want* to do. Not what I am allowed to do."

She pours him more tea. "You want to protect your brother. That is not weakness. That is love. Josiah was right."

David sits. Stares at the table. Thinks about the bond. Thinks about Amelia. The human woman who walked into a pack of shifters and made Victor *better*. He is still fighting that, not the bond itself, but what it represents. What it makes Victor. Softer. Open. Transparent. The pack can *feel* him now. They can feel his happiness, his fear, his doubt. And the pack is a strange, suspicious thing. They do not trust what they can feel. They trust what they can predict.

Victor has become unpredictable.

David takes a breath. Sets his cup down. Stands. Walks to the door. Stops. Looks back at Perseus, checking the scouts' gear. At Josiah, sitting in the corner with his eyes closed. At Emila, watching him with gentle patience.

He opens his mouth. Closes it.

"Perseus," he says.

Perseus looks up.

"If anything goes wrong with those scouts—"

"I know the rules, David. I am not going to throw my life away."

David nods. He should walk away. He should trust his brother. He should trust Perseus.

But he doesn't.

---

PERSEUS

Perseus watches the scouts leave. Ten warriors moving south along the old trade road, carrying only supplies and word. No weapons beyond their belts. No armor beyond their hides. They are brave. They are skilled. But they are ten against two hundred.

He feels the weight of the decision. Victor's decision. Not a full strike. Not silence. Something in between. A statement. A message. *We see you. We are not afraid. Do not test us.*

It is the right call. Perseus knows this. He also knows that messages do not stop armies. Intentions do not stop hollowed warriors. And Gage Hanley does not care about messages.

But Victor is the Alpha. The call is his. Perseus's job is to execute it. And to prepare for what happens next.

He turns toward the armory. Picks up a sword. Tests its weight. The battle is coming. It always is. The question is not *if*, it is *when* and *how hard*.

He goes to the training yard. Begins to move.

The work begins.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 29: The Confrontation

VICTOR

Evening settles over the compound. The war room is quiet. Papers stacked, maps rolled, lamps lit. Victor stays behind after the others have left. He sits at the head of the table, staring at the same report he has been reading for ten minutes. The bond hums in his chest, Amelia is in the healing tent, asleep, her warmth steady and slow. It grounds him. But it does not quiet his thoughts.

The door opens.

David walks in. No knocking. He closes the door behind him. The click of the latch is loud in the empty room. He does not sit. He stands on the other side of the table, arms crossed, jaw tight. His eyes are red. Not from crying. From exhaustion. From holding something in.

"I need to talk to you," David says. His voice is low. Stern.

Victor closes the report. Sets it down. Looks at his brother. "What is it?"

"It is the Hanley situation. And it is not." David shakes his head. He runs a hand through his hair. Paces two steps. Stops. "You sent ten scouts against two hundred warriors and bears. Ten." His voice is low. Controlled. But it is vibrating at the edges.

"I said it was not a full strike. It is a message."

"It is a death sentence if Hanley attacks them." David's hands curl into fists. "You know that. You know Gage does not listen to messages. He listens to force. And you sent ten men to deliver one."

Victor holds his gaze. "It is the right call."

"It is not!" David slams his hand on the table. "It is not the right call. It is the call of a man who has forgotten what war looks like. Who thinks love makes him wise. Love makes you cautious. Love makes you soft. And the pack does not need soft right now, Victor. The pack needs a warrior."

Victor stands. Slowly. The movement is deliberate. Controlled. He walks around the table until he is standing in front of his brother. Close enough to see the pulse beating in David's neck. Close enough to feel the heat radiating off him. Close enough to smell the anger.

"Sit down," David says quietly.

"I am not going to sit."

"Then listen." David's voice is steady now. The anger has burned down to something sharper. Clearer. "I respect you, Victor. I always have. Since we were children, since Father made us train, since he made me watch you take every blow so I could learn. I have followed you into every fight. I have stood behind you when you became Alpha. I have bled for this pack. And I am telling you — right now — that you are making a mistake. Not just with Hanley. With everything."

Victor waits.

David takes a breath. "The bond. The way you carry it. The way you look at her. The way you let it change you." He shakes his head. "You are the Alpha. You can't afford to be... attached to anything. To anyone. Your pack needs you focused. Present. Not distracted by some... some human who walks into our compound and turns our Alpha into a lovesick fool."

Victor feels the bond tighten in his chest. Amelia stirs in her sleep. She feels the spike in his pulse, the heat in his blood. He sends calm through the bond without thinking. *It is fine. It is just David.*

"She is not a distraction," Victor says. His voice is quiet. Low. The kind of quiet that means he is holding something back.

"She is everything." David meets his gaze. "And that is the problem. You are not the Alpha anymore. You are a mate. And the pack knows it. They can feel you through the bond. They feel your happiness. Your fear. Your doubt. They do not know what to do with it. A controlled Alpha is predictable. An open Alpha is... unsettling."

Victor feels the truth of it in his chest. The pack is watching. They are always watching. And now they see something different in him. Something they do not understand.

"I am the Alpha," Victor says. His voice does not rise. It does not need to. "I decide what I can afford. I decide what this pack needs. Not you. Not Perseus. Not the pack. Me."

David stares at him. The anger flickers — something raw and wounded underneath. "Father would not have—"

"Father is dead." Victor's voice is flat. Final. "And I am not him. I lead differently. I lead better. And if the pack cannot accept that, then they need to learn to."

David shakes his head slowly. He does not look away. He does not back down. But he nods. Once. Reluctant. Resigned. "Understood."

He turns. Walks to the door. Stops. Does not look back. "Just know this: if your love gets those scouts killed, you will carry it. Every one of them. Marcus. Tessa. Jalen. You will carry them."

The door closes behind him. The click of the latch is loud in the empty room.

Victor stands alone.

He closes his eyes. Sends the weight of his brother's words through the bond to Amelia, not the words, just the feeling. Conflict. Tension. Fear. The warmth in the bond brightens in response. She is awake now. Listening.

*I'll be okay,* he thinks. *We will be okay.*

She feels him. She feels the shape of his love. And it is enough.

---

DAVID

David walks the corridors of the compound in silence. The stone floor is cold beneath his boots. The lamps cast long shadows against the walls. He can hear the compound settling around him, the murmur of voices, the creak of timber, the distant bark of a dog. Life. Normal life. For people who are not standing at the edge of a war they do not yet understand.

He stops at the window of the corridor. Looks out into the darkness. The compound is quiet. Peaceful. He hates it.

It is not the Hanley situation that is eating at him. It is not the ten scouts. It is not even the bond itself. It is what the bond represents. It is what it has taken from Victor. The man who stood at the head of the war room table, eyes red, jaw clenched, telling his brother *I lead differently. I lead better.* He was right. David knows he was right. And that is what makes it worse.

Because Victor is right because of her. Because of Amelia. Because of the bond.

And David knows, deep in his chest, that Victor has not just changed. He has become better. Stronger. Clearer. The man who walked into the war room tonight was not the man who left it two days ago. The ceremony changed him. The bond changed him. And David is not sure if he likes what he sees.

Because the man he sees is a better Alpha. And he is not sure if a better Alpha is what the pack needs.

He remembers the first war. The one before Victor became Alpha. His father was a good Alpha. Strong. Fierce. Relentless. But he was also alone. His own Alpha had no one to lean on. No one to trust. He carried the weight of the pack on his shoulders and did not let anyone else feel it. That is what made him strong. That is what made him predictable.

And that is what killed him.

David closes his eyes. The door opens behind him. He does not turn.

"David?"

He knows the voice. Perseus. His brother's other half. The one who has always been the voice of reason. The one who has always known better than to follow impulse.

"I know you are out there," Perseus says.

David turns. Perseus stands in the corridor, arms crossed, expression unreadable. He does not approach. He waits.

"I am fine," David says.

Perseus studies him. He is good at that. Reading people. Reading situations. "You just confronted your brother."

"I told him the truth."

"He does not need truth. He needs support."

"Sometimes the truth is the support." David looks away. Looks back at the darkness outside the window. "He is making a mistake, Perseus. And I know it. And I know he is right. And that is what terrifies me."

Perseus is quiet for a long moment. Then he speaks. "You think love makes him soft."

"I think love makes him— " David stops himself. Shakes his head. "I don't know what it makes him. But I know this: the pack is watching. And if they see weakness, they will test him. And if he breaks, the Hollow King will be waiting."

Perseus nods slowly. "You want to fight him."

"I want to protect him." David looks at Perseus. Really looks at him. "I have always wanted to protect him. Since we were children. And I think I have been bad at it because I have only ever protected him from the outside. From the pack. From the enemies. But what if the real threat is what is inside him? What if the bond is the thing that gets him killed?"

Perseus meets his gaze. "Then you ask yourself: did love get him through the hollowing? Did love bring him the pack's respect after the ceremony? Did love make him stronger than he was before?" He pauses. "The bond does not make him weak, David. It makes him more than one."

David laughs. A short, bitter sound. "Since when do you sound like Josiah?"

"Since I started listening to him."

David looks away. Thinks about it. The corridor is quiet. The compound sleeps. And somewhere in the compound, his brother is carrying the weight of a pack, a bond, and a war.

"I just want him to survive," David says quietly.

"So do I," Perseus says. "So do I."

They stand in silence. Two brothers. One pack. One war. And the thin, invisible line that separates love from destruction.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 30: The Hollowing Returns

VICTOR

The scouts are gone six hours when the bond screams.

It is not a feeling. It is not a sensation. It is a rupture — a white-hot tear in the fabric of the connection that makes his vision blur and his knees buckle. He braces his palms on the war room table, breathing hard. The pack notices. Perseus stands. David steps back. Josiah's eyes open.

"Alpha?" David says.

Victor straightens. His voice is rough. Shaking. "It is happening. The Hollow King. At the border."

Perseus is already moving. "The scouts?"

Victor closes his eyes. The bond sears through him — a pain so sharp it makes his teeth ache. He feels something die. Not his scouts — something else. Something ancient and terrible. And beneath that, another pain — his scouts, screaming.

"Get the pack," Victor says. His voice is iron. "I am going to the border."

He does not wait for armor. He does not wait for a full war party. He shifts right there in the war room, bone cracking, flesh tearing, a massive wolf bursting through the door and into the corridor. David follows, already shifting. Perseus is behind them. The pack is behind them. A pack of wolves running south into the gathering dark.

---

AMELIA

She is in the healing tent. Crushing herbs. Mixing salves. The mortar feels heavy in her hand.

Then it hits.

It is not like anything she has felt before. It is not the gentle hum of the bond, the steady warmth of his presence after a long night together. This is a detonation, a blast of raw, animal power that fills her chest and burns up her throat and makes her knees buckle. She drops the mortar. It lands on the floor. Her hands are shaking. Her breath catches in her throat. She feels it, the shift. Not through words. Not through images. Through flesh. Through bone. Through instinct. She feels his muscles tensing. His spine curving. His cock thickening even as his body warps, the last echo of his human form before the wolf takes over. The wolf, the wolf is not a costume he puts on. It is something wilder. Something older. Something ancient and terrible and magnificent. It explodes through him and she feels it in her own body, like her own muscles are tensing without permission, like her teeth are sharpening, like her lungs are pulling in air that tastes like blood and storm, like her hips are aching with the phantom weight of him.

She gasps. Catches herself on the counter. The pain is overwhelming. The beauty is overwhelming. It is terrifying. It is the most beautiful thing she has ever felt.

And then it is over.

The healing tent is quiet. Her hands are shaking. The mortar lies on the floor. She can feel him through the bond — not warm. Not calm. Violent. Fierce. Alive.

She stumbles to the door. Steps outside. Looks south. The sky is dark. The air is heavy. She knows, with absolute certainty, that he is fighting.

---

VICTOR

The border is chaos.

It is a fog — thick, black, suffocating, rolling across the plain like smoke from a burning world. In the fog, shapes move. Shadows. But they are not shadows. They are things. Hollow things. Beings of smoke and bone and hunger.

The scouts are fighting. Ten warriors against something that has no number. Marcus Chen is on his back, sword in hand, slashing at a shape that does not bleed. Tessa Okafor has shifted, a silver wolf, teeth bared, eyes blazing, but she is fighting three of them. Jalen Brooks is bleeding from his shoulder. All of them are screaming.

Behind them, on the ridge, Gage Hanley and his warriors are watching. Two hundred wolves. Bears. Eyes wide with something that is not fear. Something like awe. And something like hatred.

Victor does not think. He charges.

The world narrows. The fog. The screams. The hunger. A massive wolf bursting from the tree line, he hits the fog like a storm.

The first beast meets him with claws like knives and a mouth full of teeth made from shadow. He catches it in mid-air, slams it into the ground, and tears its throat out. It dissolves into smoke. Black. Rotten.

The second beast is faster. It rakes his flank. Claws slice deep. He feels the cold — a cold that is not temperature but absence, the void itself reaching into his flesh and pulling. It hollows. He feels it in his blood. In his bones.

He turns. Snarls. The beast recoils.

That is when the second wave comes. Three more. Four more. Maybe more. The fog is alive. It is breathing. It is feeding.

And beneath it all, deeper, something vast and dark is watching. Waiting. The Hollow King. He can feel it, a presence like a black sun, drawing power from the beasts, feeding on the hollowed, growing stronger with every life it takes.

Victor howls. It is not a war cry. It is a challenge. A declaration of war against the darkness itself.

And then the rest of the pack arrives.

It is not just his wolves. It is Hanley's wolves too. Gage sees Victor fighting, truly fighting, with the ferocity of an Alpha who has nothing left to lose, and he does something unexpected. He shifts. His wolf is massive. Scarred. A veteran. He charges into the fog like a battering ram.

Behind him, two dozen Hanley warriors follow.

The tide turns.

The beasts are driven back. Pushed. The fog thins. The first beast dissolves into smoke. The second. The third. The fourth lunges at a Hanley warrior, but Gage is faster. He throws himself at it, claws raking its back, teeth tearing through shadow. It screams — a sound like metal grinding on bone, and dissolves. The fog breaks. The darkness recedes. The plain is quiet except for the groans of the wounded.

Victor shifts back. Human. Bleeding. Breathing hard.

His scouts are battered but alive. A Hanley warrior lies on his back, clutching his shoulder, eyes unfocused. He's not dying. He's hollowed, touched by the beast's cold. His body goes still.

Gage approaches. He is a man again now. Blood on his face. Fury in his eyes. He looks at the hollowed warrior. He looks at Victor. His voice is low. Fierce. But it is not directed at Victor. It is directed at the fog. At the darkness. At the thing that just killed one of his own.

"The Hollow King," Gage says. He turns his gaze to Victor. "He is real."

Victor meets his eyes. "He is. And he is not done."

Gage holds his gaze for a long moment. He has heard what the Hollow King is, Victor told his pack, and word carries fast between packs. But hearing about it and fighting it are two different things.

"Your scouts," Gage says. "They are still alive?"

"Yes."

Gage nods. Once. "Then we have work to do. The Hollow King strikes at the border. He is testing us. Testing both of us." He meets Victor's gaze. "I will not let him take more of my pack."

David steps forward. His voice is a growl. "Be careful, Gage."

"I am always careful," Gage says. He turns to his warriors. "Fall back. We move north."

He looks back at Victor one last time. "If the Hollow King pushes again, I will know." He pauses. "So will you."

Then he turns and walks away. The Hanley warriors follow. They leave the ridge. The silence settles.

Victor presses his hand to his chest. It is warm. Hot. He feels the bond in his chest, a living weight, and Amelia is there. Not with words. Not with images. Just the steady, warm, unshakable certainty that she is with him. That she always will be.

He stands alone on the ridge. The fog is gone. The wounded are being tended. The plain stretches empty in every direction. But the cold remains, in the soil, in the air, in the hollowed warrior's still body. A chill that does not belong in the world of the living.

The Hollow King has returned. And for the first time, the enemy is seen.



\pagebreak

# Chapter 31: The Aftermath

VICTOR

The hollowed warrior lies on the ground. His breathing is steady. His eyes are open. But there is nothing in them. No fear. No pain. No memory of the man who walked onto that plain an hour ago. Just a hollow shell, breathing but gone.

Victor stares at him. He wants to close those eyes. He wants to do something. Anything. But the hollowing cannot be undone. Whatever was inside that man has been consumed, absorbed into whatever vast, dark presence is waiting at the edge of the battlefield.

His hands are shaking. Not from exhaustion. From cold. A cold that has nothing to do with temperature. It is the residue of the Hollow King's power, seeping into the soil, into the air, into his own bones. He can feel it. So can Amelia. He feels her through the bond. Not words. Not images. Just a sharp ache in the back of his chest, like she can feel what he feels. The cold. The loss. The certainty that this is only the beginning.

David kneels beside the hollowed warrior. His jaw is set. His fists are clenched. Perseus stands a few feet away, staring at the tree line where the fog last clung, calculating. Josiah says nothing. He just watches Victor.

Gage Hanley stands five paces from the hollowed body. Blood on his face. A gash on his left forearm, already clotting dark. He has not looked at Victor once since he shifted back to human form. His warriors are moving. Tending to their own, gathering weapons, scanning the dark. They move like soldiers. Like professionals. Like a pack that does not panic.

"You're the Alpha," Gage says. His voice is flat. Not a question. A statement.

Victor turns to face him. "What?"

Gage nods toward the hollowed warrior. "Your Alpha. He is the leader. This is his border. His responsibility." He does not say *his failure*. Victor hears it anyway.

David steps forward. His voice is low. Dangerous. "He did his job."

Gage's eyes snap to David. "Did he? I saw him out there. He fought like a man possessed. But he was too late. My man is hollowed because this was his patrol. His watch." He pauses. "But he is not wrong." Gage's gaze flicks back to Victor. "You were not here fast enough."

The pack tenses. David's hand goes to the knife at his belt. Perseus's shoulders square. Josiah's eyes narrow.

Victor looks at Gage. He looks at the hollowed warrior. He looks at the plain where the fog rolled in like a wave, where the beasts came out of nothing and tore through ten scouts before Victor could respond. He should deny it. He should deflect. He should find a way to make this Gage's fault instead of his own.

But he cannot lie. Not to Gage's face. Not when the cold is still in his blood. Not when the bond in his chest is vibrating with the weight of every life he failed to protect.

"You are right," Victor says.

David blinks. Perseus's eyebrows rise. Josiah's mouth tightens.

Gage stares at him. Something flickers in his eyes. Surprise, maybe, or the faintest shadow of respect. "You admit it?"

"I admit it. He is alive. That is what matters. The Hollow King struck hard and fast. We will not make the same mistake twice."

Gage studies him for a long moment. The silence stretches. The wounded groan. The wind moves across the plain, carrying the smell of smoke and something sharper. Something metallic, like old blood dried into the soil.

Finally, Gage turns to his warriors. He is about to give an order. But the young wolf beside him has not taken his eyes off the hollowed body.

One of his warriors steps forward. A young wolf, barely old enough to shave, eyes wide with shock. "Alpha, he is one of ours. We cannot leave him here."

Gage looks back at Victor. "Your healers can tend to him. But he stays in my territory until he recovers. Or until he dies." He does not look at the hollowed warrior when he says it. He looks at Victor. "I do not want him on this plain again. Not hollowed. Not as bait. Not ever."

Victor nods. "I agree."

Another silence. But this one is different. Less sharp. Less hostile.

Gage turns back to his warriors. "Fall in. We ride north."

No one moves at first. Then, slowly, the Hanley warriors begin to assemble. Wolves, bears, eyes reflecting the fading light. They move with military precision. No shouting. No rushing. No panic. They are a disciplined pack. Gage has built them that way.

Gage stops. Looks back at Victor one more time.

"You fought well today," he says. "I did not expect it."

Victor meets his gaze. "I expected you to do the same."

Gage's mouth twitches. Almost a smile. Almost. "You do not know me, Alpha."

"No," Victor says. "But I want to."

Gage holds his gaze for one more beat. Then he turns and walks away.

The Hanley warriors fall in behind him. They move north. They disappear into the tree line. The plain is quiet.

Victor stands still. The cold in his bones does not recede.

---

AMELIA

She does not feel the battle. Not fully. Not after the detonation that shook her to her knees in the healing tent. But she feels everything after. The aftershocks, the echoes, the slow release of tension that radiates through the bond like a wave washing over shore.

She feels Victor shift. The way he felt before. Violent, fierce, alive. But then. Slowly. She feels him land. Not physically. Emotionally. She feels the weight of what he is carrying. Not the fight. The aftermath.

She is packing herbs into baskets when it hits her. A wave of cold that has nothing to do with the healing tent's temperature. Her hands stop moving. Her breath catches. She feels him through the bond. Not his thoughts. Not the way they are when they are together. Not the way the ceremony opens everything wide. But she feels the shape of his emotion. Heavy. Dark. Resolute.

She sets the basket down. Walks to the door. Looks south toward the border.

"He is okay," she whispers to no one. "He is okay and he is carrying it alone and I need to be there."

She does not wait for permission. She does not wait to be summoned. She grabs her satchel. The one with the dried lavender, the salve mixture, the small packet of chamomile that Victor likes because it smells like her grandmother's kitchen. And she walks.

The compound is quiet. The healing tent has just been through its own crisis. A dozen wounded shifters, all of them battered, none of them dead. Becca LaBlanc sits at the front desk, rolling bandages. She is late teens, Samuel's younger sister, an omega-in-training who normally never sits still. Today her dark eyes are tired, her movements careful, her hands steady even though she looks like she hasn't slept in days.

"Amelia," Becca says. Not surprised. Not disapproving. Just present. "You are going to find him."

It is not a question. Amelia nods. "Can you watch the tent?"

Becca rolls the last bandage tight. "I have been watching it for three hours. I can watch it for ten more minutes. Go."

Amelia does not hesitate. She steps outside. The air is cold. Not just temperature, but something deeper, something that presses against her skin like the memory of a storm. She can feel the pack in a different way than usual. Not the bond. The pack itself. The collective energy, the tension, the exhaustion. They just fought something none of them understood. And they won. But it did not feel like a win.

She walks through the compound. Past the training yards, past the barracks, past the kitchen. She takes the short route through the herb garden, the one that cuts behind the war room and leads to the second-floor quarters. The ones they share. She knows the stairs by heart, knows which one creaks, knows the hallway by the sliver of light under the door.

She climbs the stairs. Two at a time. Her heart is racing. Not from the climb. From the bond.

When she reaches the second-floor landing, she pauses. The door is closed. But she can feel him. Through the bond, through the door, through the walls. Vibrating with something she has never felt before. Not anger. Not grief. Something between them. A resonance. The bond is wide open. A bridge, not a thread. And right now it is humming with a frequency that makes her teeth ache.

She turns the handle and pushes the door open.

Footsteps. Heavy. Deliberate. Victor appears from the adjoining bathroom, a towel draped over his shoulders, water still dark in his hair. He is still wearing the clothes from the battle. Torn, stained, soaked with blood that is not his. His face is clean. His hair is wet. He has washed himself, but the cold has not left him. He looks like a man who has just walked out of a storm and cannot remember what sunlight feels like.

His eyes find hers.

The bond detonates.

Not like the ceremony. Not like the first time they touched. This is different. Quieter. Deeper. More certain. Like a key turning in a lock that has been waiting for her this whole time. She feels his surprise. She feels his relief. She feels the way his breath catches in his throat.

"Amelia," he says. His voice is rough. Low. The way it always is when he is trying not to feel too much.

"I know," she says. "I felt it. All of it."

His jaw tightens. "I told you not to worry."

"I am not worrying. I am here."

He stares at her for a long moment. Then he steps aside. "I was just —" He gestures at his half-washed face. His half-cleaned clothes.

She walks inside. The quarters are warm. Her clothes are thrown over the chair next to Victor's. The window is open a crack to let in the night air. It feels different than the night of the ceremony. Not a shrine anymore. A home.

She sets her satchel on the desk. Turns to face him.

He is standing in the center of the room. Shoulders tense. Chest rising and falling with slow, controlled breaths. The bond between them is humming. Warm, steady, vibrating with everything he is not saying.

"You are bleeding," she says.

He looks down. His left forearm is torn. A gash, three inches long, oozing dark red. It should be worse. Wolves heal fast. But the Hollow King's claws carry something else. Something that slows the healing, that leaves a trace of cold in the flesh.

"It is nothing," he says.

She walks toward him. Takes his arm. Gently. He does not pull away. He never pulls away anymore. But his muscles are rigid. Taut. Like a wire wound too tight.

She pulls a clean cloth wrapped in dried lavender from her satchel. Wrings it in a basin of water. Presses it against the wound. He flinches. Just barely. She feels it through the bond. A spike of sensation, sharp and bright, like pain and relief tangled together.

"You fought today," she says. Quietly. Not a question. A statement.

He nods. "You felt it."

"I felt the shift. I felt everything. Your anger. Your fear. Your refusal to let go." She looks up at him. "You carried ten warriors through that fog alone, Victor. And you came back."

He stares at the floor. "I was too slow."

"You came back."

He looks at her. His eyes are storm-dark. Heavy. The color of a sky that has not decided whether to break. "Gage is right. I was too slow."

Amelia stops cleaning the wound. Sets the cloth down. Takes his hand.

The bond flares. Warm, certain, steady. The bridge between them opens wider. She feels him. Everything. His guilt. His guilt about the hollowed warrior. His guilt about being too slow. His guilt about letting Gage speak to him like that in front of his pack. His guilt about everything.

And she lets him feel her back.

Not words. Not images. Just the certainty. The warmth. The refusal to let him drown in it alone.

She feels him gasp. Just barely. A small sound. A small surrender.

"I will not let you carry this alone," she says.

He looks at her. Really looks at her. Not with the Alpha's eyes. Not the eyes of a leader assessing a resource or a threat. With the eyes of a man who has just realized that the woman in front of him is stronger than he is.

"How?" he whispers.

"This way," she says. "Always this way."

She leans up. Kisses him.

Soft. Slow. Her lips against his, grounding him, the bond humming between them like a fire that refuses to go out. It is a kiss that says she is there. That she is not going anywhere. His hand comes up to her waist. His thumb presses into the fabric of her shirt. He holds her like she is the only solid thing in a world that has gone liquid.

When they break apart, he is breathing hard. Not from passion. From release. From the pressure of holding something inside for too long and finally letting it out. She rests her forehead against his. He breathes her in. For a moment the world is just this room, just the two of them, just the bond humming between them like a steady heartbeat.

Then reality settles in.

"Gage left," Victor says. His voice is low. Flat. "He does not trust me."

Amelia nods. "I know."

"But he fought. He fought alongside us. Two hundred warriors. Wolves and bears. They came."

"He saw you fight. He saw what your pack is capable of. He felt the bond. He could feel it, couldn't he?"

Victor's eyes widen. Just slightly. "Gage felt the bond?"

"The pack can feel your stress. Your fear. Your determination. The bond makes you transparent. When the fight was happening, the whole compound was vibrating. I doubt Gage didn't notice something was different about your pack."

Victor stares at her. Then he looks away. His jaw tightens. "This is temporary. The Hollow King will return. And when he does, Gage will remember that I am his rival. That I am the Alpha who hesitated."

Amelia takes his face in her hands. Forces him to look at her. "You did not hesitate. You arrived. That is what matters."

He closes his eyes. "I want this to last longer than one battle. I want the alliance to hold. But I know how this works. It never does. Packs always turn on each other when the real threat arrives."

"Then make it different," she says.

"How?"

"I don't know. I'm a human who makes salves and herbal tea. I don't know anything about pack politics or ancient wars or hollow kings." She pauses. "But I know you. And I know that you are the kind of person who changes the rules instead of following them."

He opens his eyes. Stares at her. A flicker of something in his face. Not a smile, not quite, but something close to it. Something raw. Something real.

"You think I can do this?" he asks. "Unite them? Make them see that the Hollow King is the enemy. Not each other?"

"I know you can. Because you already started today. You admitted you were too slow. You agreed to Gage's terms. You fought instead of standing on the sidelines being the Alpha who gives orders." She pauses. "I know you. And you're the one man who only changes rules when they absolutely need to be changed." She smiles faintly. "You're not the same man who brought me to the compound and told Perseus I was a witness and not a stray. You've changed. You're growing."

He almost smiles. "You remember that?"

"I remember everything."

She lets her hands fall. Steps back. The bond hums between them. Warm, steady, aching with everything neither of them is saying yet.

Victor turns to the window. Looks out at the valley. The moon is not out yet. Not high. Not silver. Not the witness it will be when the time comes. But the sky is wide and deep above the pines. Cold, clear. Stars scattered like frost on black glass. They catch on the mountain ridges and hold for a moment before fading behind the tree line.

"Gage will come back," he says. "The Hollow King will come back. And when both of them do, this alliance will either hold or it will break. I know which one is more likely."

"Victor," Amelia says.

He turns. Looks at her.

"Let me be selfish for a second," she says. "I don't care about the Hollow King. I don't care about the alliance or the pack or the Moon Goddess or any of that. I care that you are standing in this room, bleeding from a wound that will heal in two days, carrying the weight of a battle that already happened, and you are preparing yourself for a war that has not started yet." She steps toward him. Takes his hand again. "Right now, in this room, you are not the Alpha. You are not the Alpha who has to unite six shifter types. You are Victor. And I am Amelia. And right now, you are safe, and I am here, and that is enough."

He closes his eyes. His hand tightens around hers. The bond flares. Warm, certain, unshakable.

"Enough," he whispers.

And for one moment, just one, the cold in his bones recedes.

The Hollow King can wait.

He stands in the clearing.

Not the one near the northern boundary. Not the one where Josiah guided them through the ceremony. This one is older. Bigger. The grass is silver with moonlight. The air is cold and still. And ahead of him, standing beneath a great oak with branches that arc like the ribs of something ancient, she waits.

Aurela.

She looks the same as she did in his dreams. Silver hair, luminous eyes that shift between colors like moonlight on water. But she is closer now. The ceremony changed that. The bond is a bridge, and the bridge goes to her too.

"I know you are confused," she says. Her voice is not loud. It does not need to be. It carries the weight of the clearing. The weight of the moon.

"I came to you through the ceremony," Aurela says. "But now I need you to hear something you were not ready to hear before. The bond you share — the bond between you and Amelia. It is the first thread. It is a barrier. It pushes the Hollow King back. It protects your pack. But it is not enough."

"One bond is one thread," she continues. "The Hollow King cannot cross a thread. But he can slip through a gap. He can find the edges. He can work around it. He needs more threads. More bonds. A web that spans every shifter type, every territory, every border. Wolves, panthers, bears, foxes, eagles, dragons. Each bond creates a barrier. Together, they create a cage strong enough to hold him."

Victor's jaw tightens. "All of them?"

"All of them."

"You want me to find mates for every shifter type."

"No." Her eyes are soft. Sad. "I want you to make them *choose*. I do not create bonds between shifters. I do not play that game with my own children. The bonds happen on their own. But they will not happen unless the packs stop seeing each other as enemies. Unless they stop hoarding strength and start building bridges. You have to give them that chance."

Victor stares at the ground. His hands are clenched at his sides. He is an Alpha of one pack. A wolf. He barely understands his own territory. The Bear mountains are days of travel. The Panther swamps are farther. The Fox desert, the Eagle peaks, the Dragon volcanoes. Each one is a world unto itself. Each one has its own history, its own grudges, its own reasons for hating the wolves.

"I cannot do that," he says.

"You can."

"I am one man."

"You are the Alpha who admitted fault to a rival pack and formed an alliance. You are the man who performed the Moonlit Rite when he could have run. You are the one who fights instead of standing on the sidelines. You are not enough, Victor. But you are *willing*. That is the difference."

The word hits him like a blade. Willing. He has spent his entire life trying to be *enough*. Competent. Strong. Controlled. His father died because he was not fast enough. Because he hesitated. Because he thought he could calculate every outcome and come out ahead.

Willing is different. Willing does not guarantee success. Willing does not guarantee survival. Willing means showing up. It means trying. It means standing in the darkness and refusing to look away.

"How?" he says. His voice is low. Shaking. "How do I unite six shifter types that have been at each other's throats for centuries?"

"You start with the one who just fought beside you."

"Gage."

"Gage. You stood together against the Hollow King. You proved to each other that you are not enemies. That is a beginning. You keep going. You reach out to the Bear mountains. You send envoys to the Panther swamps. You find the Fox caravans. You climb the Eagle peaks. You seek the Dragon volcanoes. You do not demand allegiance. You ask for trust. One step at a time. One bond at a time."

Victor closes his eyes. He sees Gage's face on the plain. The way he shifted. The way he charged into the fog without hesitation. The way he fought like a man who does not panic. He is not wrong. He is not weak. He is exactly the kind of Alpha who would understand what Victor is trying to do. If Victor can just make him see it.

"If I fail," he says, "the Hollow King wins."

"Yes."

"If I succeed —"

"You do not know what happens if you succeed. Nobody does. The Mate Bond has happened before, but never like this. Never at this scale. I have waited centuries for this moment. I do not have a guarantee. I have a possibility. And I am betting everything on it."

Victor opens his eyes. Looks at her. "Why me? Why not one of the other Alphas? Gage is stronger. More experienced. More traditional."

Aurela's expression does not change. But something in her eyes shifts. A flicker of color, deep violet for just a heartbeat.

"Gage sees strength in control. He will try to force the packs together through power. Through fear. Through the kind of dominance that breaks before it builds. You are different. You hesitate. You question. You admit when you are wrong. You are learning. Slowly, painfully, that strength is not control. Strength is *willingness*. The willingness to trust. To open. To fail and try again. That is what this requires. Not a conqueror. A bridge-builder."

The words settle in his chest. Heavy. True. He has spent his whole life trying to be Cyrus. The Alpha who never hesitated. The Alpha who had all the answers. But Cyrus is dead. And the thing the world needs now is not another Cyrus. It is Victor. Flawed. Hesitant. Trying.

"What do I do?" he says. It is the first time he has asked Aurela for guidance. The first time he has admitted he does not know.

"Return to your pack," she says. "Return to Amelia. Hold her through the night. Tomorrow, you begin. Not with grand speeches. Not with demands. With Gage. You sit with him. You talk. You find the common ground. The rest follows."

"Common ground," Victor says. "He called my pack weak."

"He was testing you. Gage tests everyone. He does not trust easily. You earned a thread of trust today. Keep pulling it. That is all."

The clearing begins to fade. The silver grass dims. The great oak shrinks. Aurela's figure grows translucent. Moonlight becoming mist, mist becoming memory.

"Victor," she says. One last time.

He looks at her.

"You do not have to be perfect. You just have to be willing."

And she is gone.

# Chapter 33: The Vulnerability

AMELIA

She wakes up sweating.

The room is dark, the window open a crack, the night air cold. Victor is warm against her back, his arm heavy across her waist, his breathing deep, even, steady.

But she has been somewhere else.

A dark place, a cold place, a presence— a weight pressing against her mind like a hand on her chest. He was in her dreams. Not a normal dream. An invasion. He found her sleeping, found her alone, and he reached in.

She does not know how he did it. The bond should protect her. It should shield her. But the Hollow King does not attack through the bond, he attacks through the dark. He finds the cracks in the world where the Veil is thin, and he slips through.

She has never felt more exposed.

Her heart is racing, her skin is cold. The bond between her and Victor is humming—warm, steady, safe—and she clings to it like a lifeline. She feels him shift, just slightly, a stirring, a sense. She does not know if he is awake or still asleep. But she feels his awareness reaching for her through the bond. Gentle. Instinctive. A question.

She does not answer out loud. She does not wake him. She just lets him feel her— the fear, the relief, the certainty that he is there, that she is safe, that they are safe.

She closes her eyes and listens to his heartbeat—slow, steady, the kind of rhythm that says everything is going to be okay.

She almost believes it.

AMELIA

Morning light slips through the window. It is pale. Thin. The kind of light that says the sun is not ready to rise yet.

She is not.

And he did.

She does not feel afraid. Not the way she felt last night. The fear was sharp and sudden and real. But now it is fading. Replaced by something steadier. Something she does not have a name for yet.

She is bonded.

This is connection. The kind of connection that cannot be broken by distance, by fear, by the Hollow King's cold.

She is bonded.

She is not afraid.

And she is stronger than he thinks.

# Chapter 34: The Assault

AMELIA

The ward alarm sounds before she hears the beast.

A deep, resonant boom, like thunder rolling through the compound walls. It shakes the floor beneath her feet. It vibrates in her chest. The healing tent goes into chaos. Omegas scramble. Warriors shout. Pack members rush toward the walls.

Amelia does not move at first. She grips the edge of the table. Her knuckles are white. Her breath comes in short, sharp bursts. She feels the ward break through the bond. A pressure. A darkness. Something vast pressing against the perimeter of the pack's protection, tearing through it like paper.

Not a scout. Not a beast sent to test. This is the assault that should not have come yet. The coalition is not ready. There is only one bond holding the perimeter. The Hollow King is attacking before the web is complete. And they are barely holding.
